Browse All / Ganglioneuroblastoma

Ganglioneuroblastoma

MONDO:0005035 Pathograph 9 Show in embeddings browser neuroblastic tumor

Ganglioneuroblastoma is a peripheral neuroblastic tumor of childhood, of sympathoadrenal (neural crest) lineage, composed of neoplastic neuroblasts, ganglion cells and Schwannian stroma and intermediate in differentiation between neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma. The International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification (INPC) divides peripheral neuroblastic tumors into four categories, two of which are ganglioneuroblastoma. Intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is Schwannian stroma-rich and always of favorable histology. Nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is composite: one or more macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic, stroma-poor neuroblastomatous nodules sit in a stroma-rich or stroma-dominant background, the tumor is graded on its nodules, and patients are stratified with neuroblastoma risk criteria. Ganglioneuroblastoma has a malignant ICD-O-3 morphology code (9490/3, as MONDO and NCIT record it), and neither subtype is benign, although some clinical series call the intermixed subtype a benign variant (see notes). This entry covers peripheral ganglioneuroblastoma (MONDO:0005035) in both INPC categories. MONDO and NCIT define the class by a Schwannian stroma making up more than half of the tumor volume; in the INPC an over-half ganglioneuromatous component defines the intermixed category, while in a nodular tumor the proportion of neuroblastomatous nodule to intermixed or ganglioneuroma tissue varies from case to case. Central nervous system ganglioneuroblastoma (NCIT:C92629), a malignant CNS neoplasm defined without a Schwannian stroma criterion, and Hirschsprung disease-ganglioneuroblastoma syndrome (MONDO:0013082) are out of scope. MONDO and NCIT place ganglioneuroblastoma beside neuroblastoma under neuroblastic tumor, while HPO files HP:0006747 Ganglioneuroblastoma under HP:0003006 Neuroblastoma.

Mappings

MONDO
MONDO:0005035 ganglioneuroblastoma
skos:exactMatch MONDO
Primary MONDO identifier for this entry. MONDO defines the class by neuroblastic cells, ganglion cells and a Schwannian stroma making up more than half of the tumor volume, and records NCIT:C3790 and Orphanet:251877 as exact matches. The Schwannian stroma criterion matches this entry's peripheral scope; the over-half threshold corresponds to the INPC intermixed category, and the INPC sets no such threshold for the nodular category, which this entry also covers.
NCIT
NCIT:C3790 Ganglioneuroblastoma
skos:exactMatch NCIT
NCIT concept that MONDO records as an exact match of MONDO:0005035. NCIT also files Central Nervous System Ganglioneuroblastoma (NCIT:C92629) beneath it, which this entry does not cover. C3790 is used rather than the narrower Peripheral Ganglioneuroblastoma (NCIT:C6594) because NCIT files the intermixed and nodular classes (NCIT:C42057, NCIT:C42058) under C3790, while C6594 holds only the adrenal and mediastinal site classes.

Subtypes

2
Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich) MONDO:0003326 NCIT:C42057
NCIT NCIT:C42057
The INPC Schwannian stroma-rich category: a transitional form between neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma in which a ganglioneuromatous component makes up more than half of the tumor around scattered microscopic neuroblastic nests, with no macroscopic nodule. It is always classified as favorable histology. Because the category excludes a macroscopic neuroblastomatous nodule, it is typically assigned on examination of the entire resected tumor, since intermixed and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma are hard to tell apart on limited samples (the diagnosis record gives how a biopsy diagnosis is reported). Most patients have localized disease: in a CCG/COG series, 2 of 189 patients with known INSS stage had stage 4 disease. In one referral series, biopsy and resection agreed in 17 of 19 patients with localized disease who had both, and the authors judged a biopsy adequate there, but a biopsy diagnosis of intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma made alongside metastases was not confirmed at resection in 8 of 12 patients, all given chemotherapy first, whose resected tumors showed neuroblastoma. Localized disease has an excellent outcome: in the CCG/COG series no patient with a favorable-histology tumor, intermixed tumors included, died of tumor, and in the referral series none of the 41 patients with locoregional disease relapsed. In that series 6 of the 12 patients with metastases at diagnosis relapsed and 2 died of disease.
NCIT: Ganglioneuroblastoma, Intermixed (skos:exactMatch) NCIT:C42057
Show evidence (9 references)
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"GNB-Intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich) and GN (Schwannian stroma-dominant) tumors are always classified into the FH group. GNB-Nodular (composite, Schwannian stroma-rich/stroma-dominant and stroma-poor) tumors are classified into the FH or UH group based on the characteristics of the..."
In the introduction to a Children's Cancer Group and Children's Oncology Group study of 552 ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma cases, Okamatsu et al. state the INPC rule: GNB-intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich) tumors are always classified as Favorable Histology (FH), whereas GNB-nodular tumors are classified as FH or Unfavorable Histology (UH) according to the characteristics of their neuroblastic component. It directly supports this subtype's favorable histology status, in contrast to nodular tumors. The sentences restate the INPC papers, whose 2003 revision added the nodular split to the classification, rather than reporting a result of this cohort.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"| GN-maturing N (%) | GNB-intermixed N (%) | GNB-nodular FS N (%) | GNB-nodular US N (%) | ... INSS stage ... | 1, 2, 3 | 36 (100%) | 187 (99%) | 56 (95%) | 116 (47%) | | 4 | 0 (0%) | 2 (1%) | 3 (5%) | 132 (53%) | | Unknown | 7 | 9 | 0 | 4 |"
Table III of this CCG/COG series tabulates INSS stage by centrally reviewed histology; the excerpt quotes its column headings and its three stage rows. In the GNB-intermixed column, 187 patients (99%) had stage 1-3 disease, 2 (1%) had stage 4 disease and 9 had unknown stage, so 2 of the 189 (187 + 2) with known stage presented with distant metastasis. The series covers patients registered on CCG/COG neuroblastoma studies in 1991-2006, not a population-based sample.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The majority of GN-M, GNB-I, and GNB-N-FS patients had localized disease (stage 1, 2, 3) and low or intermediate-risk tumors."
In the Results of this CCG/COG series, the authors summarize Table III: most patients with ganglioneuroma-maturing, GNB-intermixed and GNB-nodular favorable-subset tumors had localized (INSS stage 1, 2 or 3) disease and low- or intermediate-risk tumors. This supports localized disease as the usual presentation of this subtype and shows that the authors class stages 1-3 as localized. The sentence pools three favorable histologies and gives no counts.

Ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite) MONDO:0003325 NCIT:C42058
NCIT NCIT:C42058
The INPC composite category: one or more macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic, Schwannian stroma-poor neuroblastomatous nodules coexist with an intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma component. The proportion of nodule to that component varies from case to case. The INPC also recognizes variants of this classical form. In one, neuroblastoma is found at a metastatic site while the primary tumor shows only intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma histology, as when a nodule in the primary was not sampled; in a CCG/COG series the unfavorable nodule was found only at the metastatic site in 6 patients even after complete pathology evaluation of the primary tumor. Very rarely, such a discordance reflects separate primary tumors presumed to be of different clones. Once classed entirely as unfavorable histology, nodular tumors are now divided into favorable and unfavorable subsets by applying the neuroblastoma criteria of age at diagnosis, grade of neuroblastic differentiation and mitosis-karyorrhexis index to the nodules. The nodular component is favorable when poorly differentiated or differentiating with a low or intermediate index in a patient younger than 18 months, or differentiating with a low index from 18 months to 5 years; it is unfavorable when undifferentiated or of high index at any age, poorly differentiated or of intermediate index from 18 months to 5 years, and of any grade or index from 5 years (60 months). Every nodular tumor diagnosed at 5 years or older is therefore in the unfavorable subset; in a CCG/COG series the favorable-subset patients were 4 months to 4 years 11 months old at diagnosis, more than half of them younger than 18 months. Umehara and colleagues defined the two subsets in 2000 from a review of 70 Children's Cancer Group patients. The International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee re-reviewed the same patients, confirmed the diagnosis in 67 and the survival difference (8-year event-free survival 86.1% in the favorable versus 32.2% in the unfavorable subset), and in 2003 proposed adding the split to the classification. An INRG analysis identified 232 patients with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma among 4,071 with a known INPC category; 83% were older than 18 months, 83% had unfavorable INPC histology and 2% had MYCN-amplified tumors. Outcome in that cohort was very heterogeneous, with event-free and overall survival of 54% and 68%, and in the unfavorable subset of a CCG/COG series 5-year event-free survival was 80.1% without and 16.7% with stage 4 disease. MONDO subdivides the class by background into MONDO:0004408 (stroma-rich) and MONDO:0004407 (stroma-dominant).
NCIT: Ganglioneuroblastoma, Nodular (skos:exactMatch) NCIT:C42058
Show evidence (14 references)
PMID:10421273 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Approximately 99% of cases (224 of 227) had consensus diagnoses for categorization: neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), 190 cases; ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich), 5 cases; ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant) maturing, 1 case; ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular..."
In a review of 227 neuroblastic tumours by the six-member International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee, consensus diagnoses (five or six of six pathologists agreeing) placed 19 cases in ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite Schwannian stroma-rich/stroma-dominant and stroma-poor) and 5 in ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich), as separate categories, supporting this subtype as distinct from the intermixed form. The counts describe this review set, not subtype frequency. The abstract gives no morphologic criteria (described separately) and presents the classification as a modified Shimada classification, so it does not show that the split originated here.
PMID:10964346 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Pathology slides and reports from a total of 70 GNBn patients from the Children's Cancer Group (CCG)-3881 and CCG-3891 studies were reviewed. Sixty-eight tumors were classified in the favorable subset (FS) or the unfavorable subset (US) based on the evaluation of nodular components by applying..."
Umehara et al. reviewed the pathology of 70 patients with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma from Children's Cancer Group studies CCG-3881 and CCG-3891. Applying the INPC neuroblastoma criteria (age, grade of differentiation, mitosis-karyorrhexis index) to the nodules, they placed 68 tumors in a favorable (22) or an unfavorable (46) subset, with event-free survival of 86.1% versus 29.0% (P < 0.0005); the sentence does not state the survival time point. This is the study that defined the subsets. The International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee re-reviewed the same patients (PMID:14601099), so the two papers analyse one cohort and are not independent results.
PMID:14601099 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Histology slides from 70 patients who were enrolled in Children's Cancer Group studies 3881 and 3891 and who had a diagnosis of GNBn were reviewed jointly by the members of International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee (INPC) ... to identify the FS and US by applying the same age-linked..."
The International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee reviewed 70 tumours diagnosed as nodular ganglioneuroblastoma in CCG studies 3881 and 3891, confirmed the diagnosis in 67, and applied neuroblastoma's age-linked histology criteria to their neuroblastomatous component, giving favourable (22) and unfavourable (45) subsets. Their 8-year event-free survival differed significantly (86.1% vs 32.2%), and the committee proposed modifying the classification to separate them. These patients had been used in a previous study: Umehara 2000 (PMID:10964346) reviewed 70 patients from the same two studies, and Okamatsu 2009 (PMID:19530234) includes them, so this is not independent confirmation.

Discussions and Knowledge Gaps

3
Is the Schwannian stroma of ganglioneuroblastoma a non-neoplastic host population recruited by the neoplastic neuroblasts, or is it derived from the neoplastic clone?
CONTROVERSY OPEN gnb_schwannian_stroma_origin
Attached to
pathophysiology#Schwannian Stromal Development
Evidence items use this convention: SUPPORT means the stroma is a non-neoplastic, recruited population; REFUTE means it derives from the neoplastic clone. For the non-neoplastic view, a 1996 study of maturing neuroblastomas found normal chromosome numbers in Schwann cells but abnormal numbers in neuroblastic and ganglionic cells, and an 8-case study of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma found the neuroblasts' MYCN gain in ganglion cells but not in Schwann cells. For the neoplastic view, a 2001 study of neuroblastomas found the same DNA content and GD2 staining in stromal and neuroblastic cells by laser-scanning cytometry in 20 tumors, and the same chromosomal makeup by fluorescence in situ hybridization in 7, and concluded that both derive from genetically identical neoplastic cells. A 2009 cooperative-group paper describes the reports as controversial. The answer decides whether ploidy and copy-number results from stroma-rich tumors describe the tumor cells, and whether maturation is an interaction between tumor and host cells.
Posed 2026-10-21T00:00:00Z
Show evidence (5 references)
PMID:8618605 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The normal number of chromosomes in Schwann cells and the abnormal number in neuroblastic ganglionic cells suggests that Schwann cells are a reactive population of normal cells that invade the neuroblastoma."
In 20 maturing and mature neuroblastomas, chromosome counts by in situ hybridization found normal numbers in Schwann cells but abnormal numbers in neuroblastic and ganglionic cells. From this contrast the authors suggest that the Schwann cells are a reactive population of normal cells that invade the tumor, which supports the view that the stroma is a non-neoplastic host population. The evidence is chromosome number alone, which by itself cannot exclude a shared clonal origin, and the abstract does not say which tumors would now be classed as ganglioneuroblastoma.
PMID:25081755 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"In contrast, the Schwann cells have a different origin and may be derived from a non-neoplastic neural crest precursor."
Supports the position that the Schwannian stroma is non-neoplastic rather than derived from the neoplastic clone. In eight nodular ganglioneuroblastomas studied by SNP array and in situ hybridization, the authors conclude that the Schwann cells, unlike the genetically related neuroblasts and ganglion cells, have a different origin, possibly a non-neoplastic neural crest precursor. Limitations: the Schwann cell result rests on in situ hybridization, because the arrays used DNA from whole neuroblastomatous and ganglioneuromatous areas, and the precursor origin is proposed, not traced.
PMID:25081755 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"By in situ hybridization, MYCN gain (4-10 gene copies/nucleus) was detected in 7/8 neuroblastoma samples. In ganglioneuromatous regions, gains were also detected in ganglion cells but not in Schwann cells."
Supports the non-neoplastic position with the observation behind it. In this series of eight nodular ganglioneuroblastomas, in situ hybridization found MYCN gain in the neuroblastoma component of seven, and in ganglioneuromatous regions in ganglion cells but not in Schwann cells. The paper reports this as a gain of 4 to 10 copies per nucleus, not as amplification. Limitations: the series is small, and lacking the gain does not by itself show that Schwann cells arise outside the neoplastic clone, the point in dispute.
+ 2 more references
Is the neuroblastomatous nodule of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma a clone genetically distinct from the rest of the tumor, or a part of a single neoplastic clone that differs from the rest only in being less differentiated?
CONTROVERSY OPEN gnb_nodule_clonality
Attached to
pathophysiology#Schwannian Stroma-Poor Neuroblastomatous Nodule has_subtypes#Nodular
Evidence items use this convention: SUPPORT means the nodule is clonally distinct (a multiclonal tumor), that is, a genetically distinct clone, whether it arose independently or as a subclone of a common ancestor; REFUTE means the nodule's neuroblasts and the ganglion cells of the ganglioneuromatous component belong to one clone and differ only in differentiation. A 2026 pathology review of the INPC describes nodular tumors as multi-clonal, and a 1993 single-case study found that the more differentiated portion and the neuroblastic foci of one composite ganglioneuroblastoma differed in DNA content and MYCN copy number. An 8-case genetic study found the nodule neuroblasts and the ganglion cells genetically related and concluded that the behavior of nodular tumors cannot be explained by separate clones with distinct genetic signatures; that argues against independent clonal origins but does not exclude a subclone that evolved from a common ancestor.
Posed 2026-10-21T00:00:00Z
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"Tumors in this category are considered as multi-clonal tumors. In gross appearance, the NB component often forms hemorrhagic and necrotic nodule(s) in a background of elastic and yellowish white component of GNB-i or GN"
In its account of the INPC categories, this 2026 pathology review states that tumours in 'this category' are considered multi-clonal, and that the neuroblastoma component often forms haemorrhagic, necrotic nodules within a ganglioneuroblastoma-intermixed or ganglioneuroma background, which identifies the category as nodular ganglioneuroblastoma. It therefore supports the multiclonal (clonally distinct nodule) position. It is an expert classification statement with no clonality data, so it records how these tumours are classified rather than showing that the nodule is a separate clone.
PMID:8108298 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"We analyzed a composite ganglioneuroblastoma for N-myc copy number at initial resection and 2 years later after progressive disease. In the second sample the more differentiated portion of the tumor was analyzed separately from the neuroblastic foci for N-myc copy number and DNA ploidy. The DNA..."
This case report analysed one composite ganglioneuroblastoma (the older name for the nodular subtype) at initial resection and again 2 years later, after progressive disease. In the second sample, the more differentiated portion and the neuroblastic foci differed in DNA content and N-myc (MYCN) copy number, which the authors read as at least two clones, supporting the clonally distinct position. It is one case, and the abstract does not say whether stromal cells were separated from tumour cells, so the two clones are an inference from two tissue portions. The abstract reports the separate analysis of the two areas only for the sample taken after progression, so it does not show whether they already differed at initial resection.
PMID:25081755 REFUTE DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Our results suggest that the clinical behavior of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma cannot be explained by the presence of separate clones with distinct genetic signatures."
Analysing eight nodular ganglioneuroblastomas by SNP array and in situ hybridization, with DNA extracted separately from neuroblastomatous and ganglioneuromatous areas, the authors suggest that the clinical behaviour of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma cannot be explained by separate clones with distinct genetic signatures. This favours the single-clone position, arguing against independent clonal origins for the neuroblastoma nodule and the ganglion cells of the ganglioneuromatous component. It neither covers the Schwann cells, which the same abstract assigns a different origin, nor excludes a nodule subclone diverging from a common ancestor; the series is small.
Does telomere maintenance mechanism status predict outcome in ganglioneuroblastoma, as it does in high-risk neuroblastoma?
KNOWLEDGE GAP OPEN gnb_tmm_prognostic_significance
Attached to
pathophysiology#Telomere Maintenance Mechanism Activation
The series that established telomere maintenance in ganglioneuroblastoma says its clinical significance there is not yet assessable, because the cohort needs several more years of follow-up. This matters for how the pathophysiology node is read. In high-risk neuroblastoma, telomere maintenance is a poor-prognostic marker and is proposed for the next International Neuroblastoma Risk Group classification, so the temptation is to carry that prognostic meaning across to ganglioneuroblastoma on the strength of the shared mechanism and the nodular subtype's comparable positive rate. Nothing in the ganglioneuroblastoma data supports that step yet. This gap is why the node has no edge to an outcome or risk node, and why one should not be added from neuroblastoma evidence.
Posed 2026-10-28T00:00:00Z
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:41858373 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"a solid evaluation of the clinical significance of TMM status in GN and GNB will require several additional years of long-term follow-up."
The authors state the gap themselves, in their discussion, about their own cohort. It is what makes this an absence of evidence rather than a negative result: they do not report that telomere maintenance fails to predict outcome in ganglioneuroblastoma, only that their follow-up is too short to say.
⚙

Pathophysiology

5
Neoplastic Neuroblast Population
The malignant neuroblastic cells of the tumor, which form the microscopic neuroblastic nests of intermixed tumors and the nodules of nodular tumors. In a 1996 series of 20 maturing and mature neuroblastomas, the neuroblastic and ganglionic cells carried numerical chromosome aberrations, with near-triploidy in 18 of 19 tumors. Flow cytometry nonetheless reads most GN-maturing and intermixed tumors as diploid (160 of 191 in a CCG/COG series), which the authors suggest may reflect the predominance of Schwannian stromal cells in the samples.
neoplastic neuroblast CL:0002676 Cell Ontology (CL) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this cell type This pathophysiological event involves neoplastic neuroblast, annotated with neural crest derived neuroblast (CL:0002676). CL:0002676 is a cell type from the Cell Ontology.
Downstream
Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation Neoplastic neuroblasts mature into ganglion cells. In nodular tumors, ganglion cells carry the MYCN gain found in the neuroblasts, while Schwann cells do not.
Schwannian Stromal Development In co-culture, neuroblastoma tumors and cell lines stimulate Schwann cell proliferation, which the authors read as a hint that neoplastic neuroblasts drive stromal growth in tumors. The evidence is in vitro and says nothing about where the stromal cells come from.
Schwannian Stroma-Poor Neuroblastomatous Nodule In 7 of 8 nodular tumors studied, the neuroblasts of the nodule carried MYCN copy gain, consistent with the nodule being formed by the neoplastic neuroblast population.
Abdominal Mass The primary tumor forms a mass at its site of origin.
Posterior Mediastinal Mass The primary tumor forms a mass at its site of origin.
Congenital Horner Syndrome Orphanet ties the clinical features of ganglioneuroblastoma, congenital Horner syndrome among them, to the location of the tumor. How the tumor produces the sign is not described.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:8618605 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Neuroblastic and ganglionic cells showed aberrations in the number of chromosomes. In situ hybridization and flow cytometry demonstrated near-trip-loidy in 18 of 19 tumors and pentaploidy in the remaining tumor."
In 20 human maturing and mature neuroblastomas (maturing neuroblastoma being the abstract's term for a tumour that differentiates toward ganglioneuroma), in situ hybridization and flow cytometry found abnormal chromosome numbers in the neuroblastic and ganglionic cells: near-triploidy in 18 of 19 tumours and pentaploidy in the remaining one. The 1996 study predates the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification and does not say how many cases would now be classed as ganglioneuroblastoma. Reading these aneuploid cells as tumour cells is an inference from the finding.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"In our series, the majority of GN-M and GNB-I tumors, even though they were biologically favorable with an excellent prognosis, had a diploid pattern determined by flow cytometry. This could be attributable to the fact that those tumors were mainly or predominantly composed of Schwannian stromal..."
In this CCG/COG series of 552 centrally reviewed ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma tumours, most GN-maturing (a ganglioneuroma subtype) and GNB-intermixed tumours had a diploid pattern by flow cytometry despite being biologically favourable; ploidy data came from COG cases only. The authors suggest, hedging with 'could be attributable', that these tumours are mainly Schwannian stroma, so neuroblastic cells were not the main cells tested. The paper reports no ploidy on separated cell populations, so this is a possible explanation, not evidence of which cells, if any, are hyperdiploid.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The majority (160/191, 84%) of GN-M and GNB-I tumors had a diploid pattern determined by flow cytometry."
The abstract of this CCG/COG series quantifies the diploid majority: 160 of the 191 GN-maturing and GNB-intermixed tumours with flow-cytometric ploidy data, all from COG cases, had a diploid pattern. The paper's Table III splits this into 28 of 32 GN-maturing (a ganglioneuroma subtype) and 132 of 159 GNB-intermixed tumours. The sentence does not say why these tumours read diploid, and it does not cover nodular tumours, whose DNA indices the paper reports as split almost evenly between diploid and hyperdiploid.
Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation
Neoplastic neuroblasts differentiate into ganglion cells. Biologically favorable peripheral neuroblastic tumors are considered to mature by age-dependent steps from neuroblastoma through intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma to ganglioneuroma.
ganglion cell CL:0011103 Cell Ontology (CL) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this cell type This pathophysiological event involves ganglion cell, annotated with sympathetic neuron (CL:0011103). CL:0011103 is a cell type from the Cell Ontology.
maturation of neuroblasts into ganglion cells GO:0030182 Gene Ontology (GO) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this biological process This pathophysiological event involves maturation of neuroblasts into ganglion cells, annotated with neuron differentiation (GO:0030182). GO:0030182 is a biological process from the Gene Ontology.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"To note, tumors in the biologically favorable pNTs are considered to proceed by age-dependent differentiation/maturational steps from NB to GNB-i, and finally to GN."
This 2026 pathology review states that biologically favourable peripheral neuroblastic tumours are considered to proceed by age-dependent differentiation and maturation steps from neuroblastoma to GNB-intermixed and finally to ganglioneuroma, the maturation this node describes. The sentence sits in the review's account of the INPC categories and gives a consensus view ('are considered to') without supporting data. It covers only the favourable path through GNB-intermixed and says nothing about how nodular tumours arise.
Schwannian Stromal Development
The Schwann-cell stroma that makes up more than half of an intermixed tumor and forms the background of a nodular one. The INPC names each category by its Schwannian stromal development, which gives intermixed tumors their elastic, yellowish-white gross appearance. In culture, neuroblastoma tumors and cell lines stimulate Schwann cell proliferation, and Schwann cell products induce neuronal differentiation of neuroblastoma cells. Whether the stromal cells are non-neoplastic cells recruited by the tumor or derive from the neoplastic clone is contested (discussion gnb_schwannian_stroma_origin).
Schwannian stromal cell CL:0002573 Cell Ontology (CL) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this cell type This pathophysiological event involves Schwannian stromal cell, annotated with Schwann cell (CL:0002573). CL:0002573 is a cell type from the Cell Ontology.
Schwann cell proliferation GO:0014010 Gene Ontology (GO) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this biological process This pathophysiological event involves Schwann cell proliferation (GO:0014010). GO:0014010 is a biological process from the Gene Ontology.
Downstream
Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation Soluble Schwann cell products induce neuronal differentiation of neuroblastoma cells. The evidence is from cultured neuroblastoma cells, not from ganglioneuroblastoma tissue.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"Tumors in this category are elastic in consistency and yellowish white in color due to Schwannian stromal development (Fig. 2A)."
In this 2026 pathology review the sentence opens the paragraph on ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed; it cites Fig. 2A, which shows the gross appearance of an adrenal intermixed tumour with a solid, elastic cut surface, and the next sentence defines the category as more than 50% ganglioneuromatous tissue. It attributes the elastic, yellowish-white gross appearance of these tumours to their Schwannian stromal development. This is the authors' descriptive synthesis rather than a measured cohort result, and the review calls ganglioneuroma elastic and yellowish white too.
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"INPC defines 4 categories in the pNTs: Neuroblastoma (NB; Schwannian stroma-poor), Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (GNB-i; Schwannian stroma-rich), Ganglioneuroma (GN; Schwannian stroma-dominant), and Ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (GNB-n; composite, Schwannian stroma-rich/stroma-dominant and..."
This 2026 pathology review restates the four INPC categories with their Schwannian stromal features; ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed is stroma-rich, and the nodular form is composite, combining stroma-rich or stroma-dominant with stroma-poor tissue. The next sentence, after an omitted citation number, says each parenthetical gives the category's unique feature of Schwannian stromal development. This supports stromal development as a defining feature of both ganglioneuroblastoma subtypes. It restates a classification rule rather than new data and does not address gross appearance.
Schwannian Stroma-Poor Neuroblastomatous Nodule
One or more macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic, Schwannian stroma-poor nodules of neuroblastoma within intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma tissue. The nodular components are not always aggressive, and the age-linked histopathology of the nodules predicts prognosis. Whether the nodule is a clone distinct from the rest of the tumor is contested (discussion gnb_nodule_clonality).
neoplastic neuroblast CL:0002676 Cell Ontology (CL) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this cell type This pathophysiological event involves neoplastic neuroblast, annotated with neural crest derived neuroblast (CL:0002676). CL:0002676 is a cell type from the Cell Ontology.
Downstream
Distant Metastasis at Diagnosis In the unfavorable subset, distant metastasis from the neuroblastomatous nodule is often already present at diagnosis, even when the primary tumor is completely resected.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:10964346 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The current study demonstrates that the nodular components in GNBn tumors are not always aggressive. The prognosis of these patients can be determined by the analysis of age-linked histopathologic features."
Conclusion of a review of pathology and outcomes for 70 patients with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma in the CCG-3881 and CCG-3891 studies. It states that the nodular components are not always aggressive and that prognosis can be determined from age-linked histopathology, defined in the Methods as the INPC neuroblastoma criteria (age, differentiation grade, mitosis-karyorrhexis index) applied to the nodules. The abstract does not separate stage from histology: 63.0% of unfavourable-subset patients presented with distant metastasis. PMID:14601099 re-reviewed the same patients and PMID:19530234 includes them, so these papers are not independent confirmation.
Telomere Maintenance Mechanism Activation
Mechanism confidence: Provisional
A subset of ganglioneuroblastoma maintains its telomeres, by either of the two routes used in neuroblastoma: alternative lengthening of telomeres, detected as C-circles, or raised TERT expression. In a Japanese series of 48 ganglioneuroblastomas, alternative lengthening was present in 6 and raised TERT expression in 7 of the 31 tested, with 12 of the 48 positive by either route. The activation is concentrated in the nodular subtype, where 10 of 21 were positive, a proportion close to the neuroblastoma figure in the same series. In the 18 ganglioneuromas, alternative lengthening was absent and one tumour had raised TERT expression. Among the positive ganglioneuroblastomas the authors report frequent ATRX alteration and infrequent TERT rearrangement, which they take to mean that TERT-high ganglioneuroblastomas raise TERT expression by some mechanism other than the rearrangement and promoter mutation that predominate in TERT-high MYCN-not-amplified neuroblastoma. Which mechanism that is, the series does not establish.

ATRX hgnc:886 HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee (hgnc) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this gene This pathophysiological event involves ATRX (hgnc:886). hgnc:886 is a gene from the HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee.

PMID:41858373 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"ALT was identified in 38 NB cases (38/255, 15%) and 6 GNB cases (6/48, 12.5%), but not in GN. High TERT expression was observed in 38% (64/169), 23% (7/31) and 14% (1/7) of NB, GNB and GN cases, respectively."
The measurement behind the node: both telomere-maintenance routes assayed in ganglioneuroblastoma tumours, with the denominators stated. Note the TERT denominators are smaller than the ALT ones, 31 of 48 ganglioneuroblastomas and 7 of 18 ganglioneuromas, so the two routes were not tested in the same number of tumours and the absence of alternative lengthening in ganglioneuroma rests on 18 cases.
PMID:41858373 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"TMM activation, defined as ALT(+) and/or high TERT expression, occurred in 12/48 GNB cases and 1/18 GN cases, particularly in the GNB-nodular type (10/21, 48%), which was similar to 39% (100/255) of NB cases."
The combined figure and the subtype concentration, which is what makes this node more than a curiosity: nearly half of nodular ganglioneuroblastomas are positive, close to the neuroblastoma proportion in the same cohort. The definition is the authors' own and is a disjunction, so a positive tumour need not have both routes. The comparison with neuroblastoma is within this one series.
PMID:41858373 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Furthermore, TMM(+) GNBs exhibited distinct features, including a high frequency of ATRX alterations and a lower frequency of TERT rearrangements."
Why ATRX is bound on this node alongside TERT. Indirect for the node's own claim: the sentence describes what distinguishes the positive tumours rather than measuring telomere maintenance, and it reports a frequency contrast without giving the counts behind it.

Histopathology

2
Schwannian Stroma-Rich Ganglioneuromatous Component With Microscopic Neuroblastic Nests
A ganglioneuromatous, Schwannian stroma-rich component that exceeds 50% of the tumor in representative sections, with scattered residual microscopic neuroblastomatous foci: pockets of neuropil containing neuroblasts, differentiating neuroblasts and ganglion cells. The tumor is maturing, but the process is incomplete. Under the INPC the favorable or unfavorable histology group is to be determined only from primary or metastatic tumor tissue obtained before chemotherapy or irradiation, and once assigned it is not to be changed during the clinical course, because therapy-associated changes, including neuroblastic differentiation and even Schwannian stromal development, may not have proven prognostic significance. A diagnosis of intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma made on a tumor resected after neoadjuvant chemotherapy does not take the effect of that chemotherapy into account.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"Ganglioneuroblastoma-Intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich): A transitional form between Neuroblastoma and Ganglioneuroma. Tumor is on its way toward full differentiation/maturation; however, the process is not complete, as evidenced by scattered "residual" microscopic neuroblastomatous foci..."
Table I of this CCG/COG clinicopathological study of 552 ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma cases restates the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification definition of the intermixed category. The definition gives the histology this finding records: a transitional form between neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma whose maturation is incomplete, with scattered 'residual' microscopic neuroblastomatous foci (pockets of neuropil holding neuroblastic cells) and a ganglioneuromatous component exceeding 50% of the total volume. It is a diagnostic criterion, not cohort data, and the 50% threshold applies to microscopic fields from representative sections, not to the whole tumour volume.
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"The distinction of FH group or UH group according to the INPC, as summarized in Table 1, should only be determined based on the pathology review of primary or metastatic tumors obtained before starting chemotherapy/irradiation therapy. Once FH or UH group classification is assigned, it should..."
This 2026 pathology review states the INPC rule immediately after noting that the neuroblastoma nodules of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma are graded by age-linked criteria and that intermixed tumors are always of favorable histology. The favorable or unfavorable histology group is to be determined only from pathology review of primary or metastatic tumors obtained before chemotherapy or irradiation starts and, once assigned, is not to be changed during the clinical course, because therapy-associated changes such as neuroblastic cytodifferentiation and even Schwannian stromal development may not have proven prognostic significance. The passage states a classification rule and reports no patient data; it concerns the histology group, not how a tumor first sampled after treatment should be categorized.
PMID:37243318 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"Diagnosing GNBI on morphological examination of the definitively resected tumor does not take into account the impact of neoadjuvant chemotherapy that might be administered before definitive tumor resection."
In the Discussion of their Memorial Sloan Kettering series, the authors note that a diagnosis of intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) made by examining the definitively resected tumor does not take into account the effect of any neoadjuvant chemotherapy given before that resection. It is a framing statement, not a result of the series, and does not say how often or in what way chemotherapy changes the histology.
Macroscopic Neuroblastomatous Nodule in a Stroma-Rich or Stroma-Dominant Background
One or more macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic, Schwannian stroma-poor neuroblastomatous nodules coexisting with an intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (stroma-rich) or ganglioneuroma (stroma-dominant) component. Under the INPC the favorable or unfavorable histology group of a nodular tumor, graded on its nodules, is to be determined only from primary or metastatic tumor tissue obtained before chemotherapy or irradiation, and once assigned it is not to be changed during the clinical course, because therapy-associated changes, including neuroblastic differentiation and even Schwannian stromal development, may not have proven prognostic significance.

PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"characterized by the presence of macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic neuroblastomatous nodule(s) of Schwannian stroma-poor component coexisting Ganglioneuroblastoma-intermixed of stroma-rich component or Ganglioneuroma of stroma-dominant component."
Table I of this CCG and COG clinicopathological series gives the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification (INPC) definitions used to classify its 552 centrally reviewed ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma cases. The quoted clause, from the Ganglioneuroblastoma-Nodular row, states each feature of this finding: one or more macroscopic, usually haemorrhagic, Schwannian stroma-poor neuroblastomatous nodules coexisting with stroma-rich ganglioneuroblastoma-intermixed or stroma-dominant ganglioneuroma. It restates the INPC definition (established 1999, revised 2003) rather than reporting a finding of this cohort, and calls the nodules usually, not always, haemorrhagic.

Abdominal Mass HP:0031500 Human Phenotype Ontology (HP) Relation: this clinical feature is this phenotype This clinical feature is abdominal mass (HP:0031500). HP:0031500 is a phenotype from the Human Phenotype Ontology.
Show evidence (2 references)
ORPHA:251877 SUPPORT INDIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"usually occurring in the posterior mediastinum, adrenal medulla and extra-adrenal retroperitoneum (but sometimes in the neck and pelvis)"
Orphanet's disease definition, which reports no patient data, names the posterior mediastinum, adrenal medulla and extra-adrenal retroperitoneum as the usual sites of ganglioneuroblastoma, and the neck and pelvis as occasional ones. A primary tumor in the adrenal medulla or retroperitoneum is an abdominal mass, but that step is inferred: the definition names sites, not a mass, and its list of clinical signs does not include one. It does not rank the sites and lists the posterior mediastinum first, so it does not show that abdominal primaries predominate.
PMID:37243318 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The primary tumor was abdominal in 39 (74%) and thoracic in 14 patients (26%)."
Retrospective Memorial Sloan Kettering series of 53 patients whose intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma was diagnosed at presentation. The primary tumor was abdominal in 39 (74%) and thoracic in 14 (26%), so most of these tumors arose in the abdomen. Reading an abdominal primary as an abdominal mass is an inference, and the paper does not name the abdominal sites. The figure covers one subtype at a tertiary referral center, where only 16 of the 53 patients were first diagnosed; it is not an estimate for ganglioneuroblastoma as a whole.

PMID:22137163 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Amongst 4071 patients in the INRG database with known INPC histological category, 232 patients with GNBn were identified. ... A cohort with superior outcome was identified: OS for GNBn patients younger than 18 months was 95%±5% (n=39) and for GNBn patients with stage 1, 2, 3, 4s was 95%±3%..."
This INRG database analysis of 232 patients with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma reports overall survival of 35% for stage 4 (n=107) and 95% for stages 1, 2, 3 and 4s (n=125); these cover all 232, so 46% had stage 4 disease. The abstract gives no stage table and never mentions metastasis, so the 46% and its reading as distant metastasis are inferences; the 46% excludes stage 4s, and no survival time point is stated. INRG pools cooperative-group data, so this cohort is not independent of the CCG/COG series also cited here.

In a review of 552 ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma cases from CCG and COG studies, 53% of patients with the unfavourable subset of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma had distant metastasis at diagnosis (Table III: 132 of 248 with known stage were stage 4; no patient had stage 4S). The figure covers that subset only; 3 of 59 favourable-subset patients were stage 4. The INRG database pools cooperative-group data including Children's Oncology Group patients, so this series probably overlaps the INRG cohort and does not independently confirm its figure.

Genetic Associations

1
MYCN Amplification (Rare Somatic Amplification)
Gene: MYCN hgnc:7559 gene page HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee (hgnc) Relation: this disease-associated gene is this gene This disease-associated gene is MYCN (hgnc:7559). hgnc:7559 is a gene from the HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee. relationship_type: BIOMARKER variant_origin: SOMATIC
Show evidence (4 references)
PMID:11745206 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"MYCN amplification was seen almost exclusively in tumors of the NB category, and no patients with tumors in either the GNBi category or in the GN category and only two patients with tumors in the GNBn category had amplified MYCN."
This Children's Cancer Group series classified 628 neuroblastic tumours under the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification and tested each for MYCN status, including 21 intermixed and 63 nodular ganglioneuroblastomas. Amplification was seen almost exclusively in the neuroblastoma category: none of the intermixed and only two of the nodular ganglioneuroblastomas were amplified. The subtype groups are small, and later cooperative-group series drawing on Children's Cancer Group studies may share patients with this one, so they do not independently confirm it.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Results of MYCN status of the individual tumors, either amplified or non-amplified ... were also collected from the patients (N=500) of both CCG and COG studies ... There were 10 patients whose tumor had amplified MYCN in this series: all 4 with FH tumors (2 cases with GN-M; 2 cases with GNB-I)..."
This CCG/COG series shows that MYCN amplification was rare: of 500 ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma patients with a MYCN result, 10 had amplified tumours, 4 with favourable histology (2 GN-maturing, 2 GNB-intermixed), all alive and well at last follow-up, and 6 with nodular unfavourable-subset (GNB-N-US) tumours, 2 of whom died of tumour. Limits: only 3 of the 4 favourable-histology patients were followed beyond a year, the GN-maturing cases are ganglioneuromas, and the series includes the 70 nodular cases of Peuchmaur et al. (2003), so it is not independent of other CCG reports.
PMID:11745206 SUPPORT INDIRECT BACKGROUND Human Clinical
"MYCN amplification is a known molecular marker for aggressive progression of NTs. These have been used together as important prognostic factors to define risk groups for patient stratification and protocol assignment."
The background of this Children's Cancer Group study states that MYCN amplification is a known marker of aggressive progression in neuroblastic tumors, and that it is used together with INPC histology, the other factor 'These' refers to, to define risk groups for patient stratification and protocol assignment. That is the prognostic use behind this record's BIOMARKER typing. It is said of neuroblastic tumors as a family, most of them neuroblastomas, not of ganglioneuroblastoma alone.

Medical Actions

3
Watchful Observation
Category: Monitoring Action: watchful active observationNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is watchful active observation, annotated with Observation Activity (NCIT:C25598). NCIT:C25598 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Observation Activity NCIT:C25598
For non-metastatic intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma, studied together in retrospective series whose abstracts report no outcome for observed intermixed tumors on their own, a UK nationwide series concluded that watchful active observation can be a safe approach, with surgical resection reserved for symptomatic patients, and a single-center series that these tumors grow slowly and resection can carry significant morbidity, so watch-and-wait approaches should be considered for some patients. Observation rests on the biopsy diagnosis, which is not always upheld at surgery: in the UK series, 14 cases (12%) diagnosed on biopsy as ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma had neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma on surgical pathology, and a small proportion of observed patients had their diagnosis revised at surgery.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:39582110 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Watchful active observation can be a safe approach, with surgical resection reserved for symptomatic patients. However, a small proportion of patients in the observation group had their diagnosis revised to malignant at surgery."
This UK CCLG nationwide retrospective review covers 259 patients with histologically confirmed non-metastatic ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma. It concludes that observation can be safe, with resection reserved for symptomatic patients, though a small proportion of observed patients had their diagnosis revised at surgery. The abstract calls that a revision to malignant, and its background describes both tumors as benign variants, whereas ICD-O-3 codes ganglioneuroblastoma as malignant (9490/3). The two diagnoses are pooled and survival is not reported by management arm, so the safety statement is the authors' judgement. Twenty-one of the 58 observed patients (36%) later needed surgery, with progressive tumour growth cited as a reason; how many of them were reclassified is not stated.
PMID:39582110 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"In 45 cases (39%), surgical pathology was different from pathology at biopsy, and in 14 (12%) the pathology changed from GN/GNBi to neuroblastoma/GNB nodular."
In this UK CCLG retrospective series of 259 patients with non-metastatic ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, surgical pathology differed from the biopsy diagnosis in 45 cases, and in 14 (12%) a biopsy diagnosis of ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma became neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma at surgery. The abstract gives no denominator for the percentages, does not split the changes by biopsy diagnosis or management arm, and does not say whether they reflect limited sampling, reinterpretation or change in the tumor before surgery.
PMID:29369484 SUPPORT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"GN and GNB-I have a slow growth rate and resection can be associated with significant morbidity. Watch and wait approaches should be considered for some GN and GNB-I."
This single-centre retrospective cohort (Hospital for Sick Children, 1990-2014) covers 67 patients with ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (17 intermixed), 21 of them observed. Its conclusion recommends considering watch-and-wait for some patients, citing slow tumour growth and the significant morbidity resection can carry. It supports this rationale and recommendation only; metastatic status is not stated, and 6 of the 21 observed patients had progressive disease. Pathology changed at resection in three cases, but the abstract says only that no ganglioneuroma became neuroblastoma, so it does not show the revisions to neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma reported in the UK series.
Surgical Resection
Category: Therapeutic Action: surgical resectionNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is surgical resection, annotated with Tumor Resection (NCIT:C164212). NCIT:C164212 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Tumor Resection NCIT:C164212
Platform: Surgery
In localized intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, studied together with ganglioneuroma, chemotherapy seemed not to be effective and incomplete resection leaving only minor residuals (under 2 cm) appeared sufficient. In nodular ganglioneuroblastoma of the unfavorable subset, a CCG/COG series found no significant survival difference between complete primary tumor resection (at least 95% removed before chemotherapy or irradiation) and biopsy or partial resection, in patients with or without distant metastasis. The non-significant differences favored complete resection, the non-stage 4 comparison included few incompletely resected patients, and prognosis depended significantly on stage.
Mechanism Target:
INHIBITS Neoplastic Neuroblast Population — Resection removes all or most of the primary tumor together with its neoplastic neuroblasts. In a German series of localized intermixed tumors and ganglioneuroma, even incomplete resection leaving minor residuals (under 2 cm) appeared sufficient treatment. How resection affects outcome in nodular disease is described in this treatment's description.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Outcome was excellent and chemotherapy seems not effective while incomplete resection with minor residuals (<2 cm) appears to be sufficient for treatment."
In this retrospective study of localized ganglioneuroma and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma registered in the German NB97 and NB2004 trials, the authors conclude that resection, even when it left residuals under 2 cm, appears sufficient treatment, while chemotherapy seems not effective. This is a treatment outcome, so reading it as resection reducing the neoplastic neuroblast population is an inference. No tumor progressed after complete resection or with residuals under 2 cm, so control did not depend on removing the whole tumor. The sentence pools the two tumor types.
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"However, patients with major residuals (>2 cm) had a worse EFS than patients with complete tumor resection or minor residuals as well for the whole group of mature NT (p < 0.001; Fig. 4a) as for the groups of GN (p = 0.001; Fig. 5a) and GNBI seen individually (p = 0.005; Fig. 6a)."
In the same German series, patients left with residual tumor over 2 cm had worse event-free survival than those with complete resection or minor residuals, and this held for intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma on its own (p = 0.005). More tumor left behind went with more events: all five local progressions, two of them in intermixed tumors, arose in residuals over 2 cm. That this reflects resection reducing the tumor is an inference from outcome, and overall survival was not influenced by the extent of initial surgery.
Show evidence (4 references)
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Outcome was excellent and chemotherapy seems not effective while incomplete resection with minor residuals (<2 cm) appears to be sufficient for treatment."
This summarises a retrospective study of localized tumours registered in the German neuroblastoma trials NB97 and NB2004, including 162 ganglioneuroma and 55 intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) patients. None of the 11 patients given chemotherapy, 9 with GNBI, reached a complete or very good partial response. No tumour of either type progressed after complete resection or with residuals under 2 cm, whereas all five progressions had residuals over 2 cm. Few patients received chemotherapy, and the authors allow that single GNBI patients with immature regions in large residuals might benefit.
PMID:41568977 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Four patients received chemotherapy but showed no significant tumor reduction postchemotherapy, and all required surgery afterward."
This abstract reports a retrospective single-centre series of 12 patients with nonmetastatic intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma at Phoenix Children's Hospital. The four patients given chemotherapy showed no significant tumour reduction and all still needed surgery, and chemotherapy caused febrile neutropenia and an anaphylactic reaction. The title and conclusion frame these tumours as unresectable, but the abstract does not say why each patient received chemotherapy. With only four treated patients the evidence is thin, and it says nothing about how complete a resection must be.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Complete primary tumor resection was not beneficial to those GNB-N-US patients, regardless of whether metastasis was present or not."
In a retrospective CCG/COG cohort, the authors report that complete primary resection was not beneficial in nodular unfavourable-subset ganglioneuroblastoma (GNB-N-US), with or without distant metastasis. The analysis compared complete resection (at least 95% removed before chemotherapy or irradiation) with biopsy or partial resection in 226 patients otherwise treated by risk group. Differences were not significant but favoured complete resection (5-year EFS 83.4% vs 60.6%, p = 0.2227, non-stage 4, only 16 incompletely resected; 24.3% vs 5.4%, p = 0.1068, stage 4), so benefit is not excluded.
+ 1 more reference
Risk-Adapted Chemotherapy-Based Therapy
Category: Therapeutic Action: risk-adapted chemotherapy within neuroblastoma risk-group protocolsNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is risk-adapted chemotherapy within neuroblastoma risk-group protocols, annotated with Chemotherapy (NCIT:C15632). NCIT:C15632 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Chemotherapy NCIT:C15632
Patients are treated by neuroblastoma risk group. In Children's Cancer Group and Children's Oncology Group studies that registered patients from 1991 to 2006, low-risk patients had surgery alone, intermediate-risk patients moderate chemotherapy with surgery, and high-risk patients intensive chemotherapy, surgery, radiotherapy and often myeloablative therapy. In one referral series of intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma diagnosed at presentation (1995-2021), the 12 patients presenting with metastases, all diagnosed on biopsy, were treated as high-risk neuroblastoma with high-dose chemotherapy, radiotherapy and anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody immunotherapy. Of the 41 with locoregional disease, 11 received intermediate-risk neoadjuvant chemotherapy and 30 no cytotoxic therapy, and all 41 remained progression-free at a median follow-up of 4 years; in localized intermixed tumors chemotherapy has seemed ineffective (see Surgical Resection). Patients with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma older than 18 months with stage 4 disease form a high-risk subgroup that should be considered for aggressive upfront treatment.

The Methods of this CCG/COG series of 552 ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma cases, registered on neuroblastoma studies from August 1991 to December 2006, state how the patients were treated: by neuroblastoma risk group, with surgery alone for low risk, moderate chemotherapy with surgery for intermediate risk, and intensive chemotherapy, surgery, radiotherapy and often myeloablative therapy for high risk. These are the regimens of that period. It describes the protocols applied, not a comparison of treatments, and about 60% of the patients (329 of 552) were enrolled on therapeutic studies.
PMID:22137163 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Similar to the overall NB cohort, patients with GNBn older than 18 months of age, with stage 4 disease represent a high-risk sub-group and should be considered for aggressive treatment upfront."
In an analysis of the INRG database, Angelini and colleagues identified 232 patients with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma among 4071 with a known INPC category. They conclude that, as in neuroblastoma overall, those older than 18 months with stage 4 disease are a high-risk subgroup who should be considered for aggressive upfront treatment. The recommendation follows from survival outcomes, not a comparison of treatments, and names no specific regimen. The abstract reports overall survival for all stage 4 patients (35%, n=107), not separately for those over 18 months.
PMID:37243318 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"All patients with metastatic disease were diagnosed at >18 months of age, were deemed to have high-risk NB, and were treated with multimodality therapy including high-dose chemotherapy, radiotherapy and anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody-mediated immunotherapy."
In this retrospective Memorial Sloan Kettering series of intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma diagnosed at presentation (1995-2021), all 12 patients with metastatic disease were older than 18 months, were treated as high-risk neuroblastoma, and received multimodality therapy including high-dose chemotherapy, radiotherapy and anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody immunotherapy. Table 2 shows that all 12 were diagnosed on biopsy, and 8 of their 12 resections, after chemotherapy, showed neuroblastoma. The paper names no antibody and gives no treatment dates; it reports one center's practice, not a comparison of treatments.

Diagnosis

1
Histopathologic Classification
Ganglioneuroblastoma is diagnosed and subtyped by histopathologic examination of tumor tissue under the INPC. Because a macroscopic neuroblastomatous nodule separates nodular from intermixed tumors, which are hard to tell apart on limited samples, the completely resected tumor is the ideal sample. When only a biopsy or an incompletely resected tumor is available, a favorable classification is reported as based on review of limited material: the CCG/COG central reviewers did so, and a 2026 pathology review advises it for core needle biopsies that show only intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma.
histopathologic examination under the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification NCIT:C18190 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:37243318 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"The diagnosis of GNBI is typically made on a diligent morphological examination of the entire resected tumor because distinguishing GNBI from GNBN is particularly challenging on limited samples."
The Discussion of this Memorial Sloan Kettering series opens by restating established practice: intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) is usually diagnosed on careful examination of the entire resected tumor, because it is particularly hard to tell from nodular ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBN) on limited samples. It states practice and reports no data from the series.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT Human Clinical
"It was always ideal for pathologist to have a completely resected sample for the final diagnosis, so as not to miss any unfavorable nodules or different clones. However, in real life, we sometimes encountered the situation/clinical decision where we had to evaluate a biopsy or incompletely..."
In the Discussion of this CCG/COG series, the central review pathologists state that a completely resected sample was always the ideal basis for the final diagnosis, so that no unfavorable nodule or different clone was missed. When only a biopsy or an incompletely resected tumor was available, a favorable diagnosis of GN-maturing, GNB-intermixed or favorable-subset GNB-nodular carried the remark that it was based on review of limited material. This describes the series' diagnostic practice, not an outcome.
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"especially when the samples obtained by PCNB show only GNB-i or GN histology, it is advisable to include a note of "Favorable Histology of this case according to the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification is determined based on review of limited material" in the surgical pathology report."
This 2026 pathology review advises, as the authors' routine practice, that when percutaneous core needle biopsy (PCNB) samples show only GNB-intermixed or ganglioneuroma histology, the report should note that the favorable INPC classification was determined on review of limited material. The preceding sentence gives the reason: a core needle biopsy can miss the neuroblastoma nodule of a nodular ganglioneuroblastoma. It is an expert recommendation about core needle biopsy, without case data.

Epidemiology

1
Age at diagnosis
In a Children's Cancer Group pathology review of 746 neuroblastic tumors from studies CCG-3881 and CCG-3891, the median age at diagnosis was 3.51 years (range 0.96-14.85) for intermixed and 3.26 years (range 0.57-15.90) for nodular ganglioneuroblastoma. A larger Children's Cancer Group and Children's Oncology Group series, which overlaps this cohort, gives median ages of 4 years 1 month for 198 intermixed tumors, and of 1 year 5 months for 59 favorable-subset and 3 years 9 months for 252 unfavorable-subset nodular tumors.

In contrast, patients with UH tumors generally were older when they were diagnosed, and with median ages of ......"
This Children's Cancer Group review of 746 neuroblastic tumours from studies CCG-3881 and CCG-3891 reports median age at diagnosis of 3.51 years (range 0.96-14.85) for intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (30 tumours, all favourable histology) and 3.26 years (range 0.57-15.90) for nodular ganglioneuroblastoma (76 tumours, all unfavourable histology in this analysis). The 4.80-year median is for ganglioneuroma, and the omitted text lists only neuroblastoma-subtype medians. The quoted remark that unfavourable-histology patients were generally older does not hold here, as the nodular median is the lower. These are cooperative-group study patients, not a population sample.

🔀

Differential Diagnoses

2

Conditions with similar clinical presentations that must be differentiated from Ganglioneuroblastoma:

Ganglioneuroma MONDO:0005033
Overlapping Features Ganglioneuroma is the Schwannian stroma-dominant category of the peripheral neuroblastic tumors, and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is the Schwannian stroma-rich category beside it. Both consist largely of Schwannian stroma containing mature or maturing ganglion cells, and the INPC maturing subtype of ganglioneuroma may also hold a minor component of individually scattered differentiating neuroblasts. Read side by side, their INPC definitions differ in the well-defined microscopic neuroblastic nests that characterize the intermixed category; differentiating neuroblasts scattered as single cells are compatible with maturing ganglioneuroma. For decades ganglioneuroblastoma caused some consternation for pathologists and oncologists, one reason being that there was no clear and objective delineation between it and ganglioneuroma. The INPC maturing subtype of ganglioneuroma is the tumor group that the earlier Shimada classification called ganglioneuroblastoma, well differentiated, so a tumor reported as well-differentiated ganglioneuroblastoma under the Shimada nomenclature is a ganglioneuroma under the INPC. Sampling adds another source of confusion: compared with surgical resection, core needle biopsy seems to be at a disadvantage with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, in which a neuroblastoma nodule coexists with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma tissue.
Distinguishing Features
  • Intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is defined as tumor tissue more than 50% occupied by a ganglioneuromatous component and is characterized by well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells whose neurites are segmentally naked within the nests.
  • In ganglioneuroma, ganglion cells lie individually or in small clusters in Schwannian stroma and are either all completely mature (mature subtype) or a mixture of mature and maturing cells (maturing subtype); completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells, and naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells.
  • Under the INPC definition the maturing subtype of ganglioneuroma may also contain a minor component of individually scattered differentiating neuroblasts, present as single scattered cells rather than in the microscopic neuroblastic nests that characterize intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma.
  • Under the INPC, intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is Schwannian stroma-rich and ganglioneuroma Schwannian stroma-dominant, while nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is a composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue.
  • Compared with surgical resection, core needle biopsy seems to be at a disadvantage with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, where a neuroblastoma nodule coexists with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma tissue; when the tumor is large, multiple cores may give a better chance than incisional biopsy of sampling the nodule, which is often hemorrhagic or necrotic and detected on radiological images.
Show evidence (5 references)
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"By definition, more than 50% of the tumor tissue in this category is occupied by a ganglioneuromatous component (see below), where well-developed Schwannian stroma contains sporadic mature or maturing ganglion cells. The tumors are characterized by the presence of well-defined microscopic nests..."
In this 2026 pathology review, part one of the quote comes from the section headed 'Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich)' and says only 'this category'; the Fig. 2B legend it cites labels the image as ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed. Part two comes from the section headed 'Ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant)' and names its category through the mature and maturing GN subtypes. Restating the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification, the review defines intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma as tumor tissue more than 50% occupied by a ganglioneuromatous component and characterizes it by well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells whose neurites are segmentally naked within the nests. In ganglioneuroma, ganglion cells lie individually or in small clusters in Schwannian stroma and are either completely mature (mature GN) or a mixture of mature and maturing cells (maturing GN); completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells, and because their neurites are immediately incorporated into Schwannian stromal cells, naked neurites are not identified around them. The review describes the two categories in separate paragraphs and does not compare them directly; read side by side, the definitions set the neuroblastic nests of the intermixed tumor against the individually distributed or clustered ganglion cells of ganglioneuroma. These are classification criteria, not case data.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"Ganglioneuroma-Maturing (Schwannian stroma-dominant): Composed predominantly of ganglioneuromatous stroma with a minor component of individually scattered, evenly or unevenly distributed differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells, in addition to fully mature ganglion cells. Fully..."
Table I of this CCG/COG clinicopathological series restates the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification definition of the ganglioneuroma, maturing subtype (Schwannian stroma-dominant): mostly ganglioneuromatous stroma with a minor component of individually scattered, evenly or unevenly distributed differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells, beside fully mature ganglion cells that are always covered with satellite cells. So differentiating neuroblasts scattered as single cells can occur in this ganglioneuroma subtype. The intermixed row of the same table instead describes scattered 'residual' microscopic neuroblastomatous foci presenting as pockets of neuropil; the contrast between single scattered cells and those foci is read from the two definitions side by side and is not stated in this row. It is a classification rule restated from the INPC papers, not a result of this cohort.
PMID:10421272 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3)..."
This is the 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee, whose six member pathologists defined morphologically based classifications on the basis of a review of 227 cases. It classifies neuroblastic tumors into four categories, each labeled by its Schwannian stroma: neuroblastoma (stroma-poor), ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (stroma-rich), ganglioneuroma (stroma-dominant, with maturing and mature subtypes) and ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular, a composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue. The scheme therefore separates ganglioneuroblastoma from stroma-poor neuroblastoma and from stroma-dominant ganglioneuroma. The abstract gives each category its name, its Schwannian stromal label and, for neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma, the names of its subtypes, but not the morphologic criteria that the article says it defines. 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant' is the source's typo for stroma-rich/stroma-dominant, the spelling in the abstract of the companion INPC article in the same issue (PMID:10421273). In this abstract INPC abbreviates the Committee, which proposes the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification.
+ 2 more references
Neuroblastoma MONDO:0005072
Overlapping Features Neuroblastoma is the Schwannian stroma-poor category of the same INPC classification, and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is a composite of stroma-poor neuroblastoma tissue with stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue, so different samples from one patient can be assigned different categories. When core needle biopsy samples from one site show intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma and another site in the same patient shows neuroblastoma, a 2026 pathology review advises considering a variant of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, in which a neuroblastoma nodule in the primary tumor was not sampled but is found at the metastatic site, or, extremely rarely, multiple primaries presumably composed of different clones. In one tertiary-center retrospective series, 12 patients with stage M disease had intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma diagnosed on biopsy; after neoadjuvant chemotherapy the definitive resection showed neuroblastoma in 8 (67%), so the biopsy diagnosis could not be corroborated, and the authors advise high caution with a biopsy diagnosis of intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma when metastases are present.
Distinguishing Features
  • Histologically, tumors in the INPC neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor) category are composed of neuroblastic cells in sheets or nests demarcated by thin fibrovascular septa, with limited or absent Schwannian stromal development.
  • Under the INPC, neuroblastoma is Schwannian stroma-poor, intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma Schwannian stroma-rich, and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma a composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue.
  • Under the INPC definition, intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is a transitional form between neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma with scattered residual microscopic neuroblastomatous foci presenting as pockets of neuropil, and its ganglioneuromatous component should exceed 50% of the total volume in microscopic fields from representative sections of the tumor.
  • Nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is characterized by macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic neuroblastomatous nodules of Schwannian stroma-poor component coexisting with stroma-rich intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or stroma-dominant ganglioneuroma.
  • When core needle biopsy samples show intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma at one site and neuroblastoma at another in the same patient, a 2026 pathology review advises considering a variant of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma whose primary-site neuroblastoma nodule was not sampled but is found at the metastatic site or, extremely rarely, multiple primaries presumably composed of different clones.
  • In one tertiary-center series, resection after neoadjuvant chemotherapy showed neuroblastoma in 8 of 12 patients with stage M disease whose biopsy had shown intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, and the authors advise high caution with such a biopsy diagnosis when metastases are present.

PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
