Source YAML

click to show 
name: Ganglioneuroblastoma
creation_date: '2026-10-21T13:46:41Z'
description: >-
  Ganglioneuroblastoma is a peripheral neuroblastic tumor of childhood, of
  sympathoadrenal (neural crest) lineage, composed of neoplastic neuroblasts,
  ganglion cells and Schwannian stroma and intermediate in differentiation
  between neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma. The International Neuroblastoma
  Pathology Classification (INPC) divides peripheral neuroblastic tumors into
  four categories, two of which are ganglioneuroblastoma. Intermixed
  ganglioneuroblastoma is Schwannian stroma-rich and always of favorable
  histology. Nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is composite: one or more macroscopic,
  usually hemorrhagic, stroma-poor neuroblastomatous nodules sit in a
  stroma-rich or stroma-dominant background, the tumor is graded on its nodules,
  and patients are stratified with neuroblastoma risk criteria.
  Ganglioneuroblastoma has a malignant ICD-O-3 morphology code (9490/3, as MONDO
  and NCIT record it), and neither subtype is benign, although some clinical
  series call the intermixed subtype a benign variant (see notes). This entry
  covers peripheral ganglioneuroblastoma (MONDO:0005035) in both INPC
  categories. MONDO and NCIT define the class by a Schwannian stroma making up
  more than half of the tumor volume; in the INPC an over-half
  ganglioneuromatous component defines the intermixed category, while in a
  nodular tumor the proportion of neuroblastomatous nodule to intermixed or
  ganglioneuroma tissue varies from case to case. Central nervous system
  ganglioneuroblastoma (NCIT:C92629), a malignant CNS neoplasm defined without a
  Schwannian stroma criterion, and Hirschsprung disease-ganglioneuroblastoma
  syndrome (MONDO:0013082) are out of scope. MONDO and NCIT place
  ganglioneuroblastoma beside neuroblastoma under neuroblastic tumor, while HPO
  files HP:0006747 Ganglioneuroblastoma under HP:0003006 Neuroblastoma.
categories:
- Pediatric Cancer
- Neural Tumor
- Solid Tumor
parents:
- neuroblastic tumor
synonyms:
- ganglioneuroblastoma, malignant
- GNB
notes: >-
  The WHO Classification of Tumours, Paediatric Tumours (5th edition) lists
  ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed and ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (and other
  composite neuroblastic tumours) as separate entries beside neuroblastoma and
  ganglioneuroma; the volume is not indexed in PubMed, so it is not cited as
  evidence. NCIT defines ganglioneuroblastoma as a malignant neuroblastic tumor.
  Usage for the intermixed subtype is not uniform in the cited series: the UK
  CCLG nationwide study (Burnand 2025) calls intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma and
  ganglioneuroma benign variants of peripheral neuroblastic tumors, and its
  remark that some observed patients had their diagnosis revised to malignant at
  surgery follows that usage, while the German trial series (Decarolis 2016)
  calls both mature variants and notes that ganglioneuroma is generally
  considered benign whereas intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is widely seen as a
  malignant entity. The INPC's favorable histology, which every intermixed tumor
  has, is a prognostic grouping and does not make the tumor benign.
  MONDO:0003327 peripheral ganglioneuroblastoma has the same scope as this entry
  (MONDO's editors removed its logical definition because it made the class
  equivalent to its parent), and the site classes adrenal gland
  ganglioneuroblastoma (MONDO:0004477) and mediastinum ganglioneuroblastoma
  (MONDO:0001096) fall within it, although MONDO also files them under the
  matching neuroblastoma site classes. INRG and Children's Oncology Group
  cohorts often pool ganglioneuroblastoma with neuroblastoma, so
  neuroblastoma-wide figures may include it. Several cooperative-group series
  cited here share patients: Umehara 2000 and Peuchmaur 2003 analysed the same
  70 nodular patients from studies CCG-3881 and CCG-3891, Okamatsu 2009 includes
  them, and Goto 2001 and Shimada 2001 are also Children's Cancer Group series.
  Germline predisposition is not modelled in this entry: Orphanet lists ALK as a
  major susceptibility factor for ganglioneuroblastoma, and MONDO treats
  ALK-related predisposition as a separate disease, neuroblastoma,
  susceptibility to, 3 (MONDO:0013083).
epidemiology:
- name: Age at diagnosis
  description: >-
    In a Children's Cancer Group pathology review of 746 neuroblastic tumors
    from studies CCG-3881 and CCG-3891, the median age at diagnosis was 3.51
    years (range 0.96-14.85) for intermixed and 3.26 years (range 0.57-15.90)
    for nodular ganglioneuroblastoma. A larger Children's Cancer Group and
    Children's Oncology Group series, which overlaps this cohort, gives median
    ages of 4 years 1 month for 198 intermixed tumors, and of 1 year 5 months
    for 59 favorable-subset and 3 years 9 months for 252 unfavorable-subset
    nodular tumors.

has_subtypes:
- name: Intermixed
  display_name: "Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich)"
  classification: histological
  subtype_term:
    preferred_term: intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma
    term:
      id: MONDO:0003326
      label: intermixed schwannian stroma-rich ganglioneuroblastoma
  mappings:
    ncit_mappings:
    - term:
        id: NCIT:C42057
        label: "Ganglioneuroblastoma, Intermixed"
      mapping_predicate: skos:exactMatch
      mapping_source: NCIT
      mapping_justification: >-
        NCIT concept that MONDO records as an exact match of MONDO:0003326.
  description: >-
    The INPC Schwannian stroma-rich category: a transitional form between
    neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma in which a ganglioneuromatous component
    makes up more than half of the tumor around scattered microscopic
    neuroblastic nests, with no macroscopic nodule. It is always classified as
    favorable histology. Because the category excludes a macroscopic
    neuroblastomatous nodule, it is typically assigned on examination of the
    entire resected tumor, since intermixed and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma are
    hard to tell apart on limited samples (the diagnosis record gives how a
    biopsy diagnosis is reported). Most patients have localized disease: in a
    CCG/COG series, 2 of 189 patients with known INSS stage had stage 4 disease.
    In one referral series, biopsy and resection agreed in 17 of 19 patients
    with localized disease who had both, and the authors judged a biopsy
    adequate there, but a biopsy diagnosis of intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma
    made alongside metastases was not confirmed at resection in 8 of 12
    patients, all given chemotherapy first, whose resected tumors showed
    neuroblastoma. Localized disease has an excellent outcome: in the CCG/COG
    series no patient with a favorable-histology tumor, intermixed tumors
    included, died of tumor, and in the referral series none of the 41 patients
    with locoregional disease relapsed. In that series 6 of the 12 patients with
    metastases at diagnosis relapsed and 2 died of disease.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "GNB-Intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich) and GN (Schwannian stroma-dominant) tumors are always classified into the FH group. GNB-Nodular (composite, Schwannian stroma-rich/stroma-dominant and stroma-poor) tumors are classified into the FH or UH group based on the characteristics of the neuroblastic component."
    explanation: >-
      In the introduction to a Children's Cancer Group and Children's Oncology
      Group study of 552 ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma cases, Okamatsu
      et al. state the INPC rule: GNB-intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich) tumors
      are always classified as Favorable Histology (FH), whereas GNB-nodular
      tumors are classified as FH or Unfavorable Histology (UH) according to the
      characteristics of their neuroblastic component. It directly supports this
      subtype's favorable histology status, in contrast to nodular tumors. The
      sentences restate the INPC papers, whose 2003 revision added the nodular
      split to the classification, rather than reporting a result of this
      cohort.

    reference_title: "Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed: Clinicopathological implications of diagnosis at presentation and genomic correlations."

    explanation: >-
      In a retrospective Memorial Sloan Kettering archive review (15,827
      neuroblastoma specimens), 53 patients had intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma
      (GNBI) at initial diagnosis, each confirmed on review at that centre. All
      12 with stage M disease were diagnosed on biopsy and received neoadjuvant

- name: Nodular
  display_name: "Ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite)"
  classification: histological
  subtype_term:
    preferred_term: nodular ganglioneuroblastoma
    term:
      id: MONDO:0003325
      label: nodular ganglioneuroblastoma
  mappings:
    ncit_mappings:
    - term:
        id: NCIT:C42058
        label: "Ganglioneuroblastoma, Nodular"
      mapping_predicate: skos:exactMatch
      mapping_source: NCIT
      mapping_justification: >-
        NCIT concept that MONDO records as an exact match of MONDO:0003325.
  description: >-
    The INPC composite category: one or more macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic,
    Schwannian stroma-poor neuroblastomatous nodules coexist with an intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma component. The proportion of nodule
    to that component varies from case to case. The INPC also recognizes
    variants of this classical form. In one, neuroblastoma is found at a
    metastatic site while the primary tumor shows only intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma histology, as when a nodule in the
    primary was not sampled; in a CCG/COG series the unfavorable nodule was
    found only at the metastatic site in 6 patients even after complete
    pathology evaluation of the primary tumor. Very rarely, such a discordance
    reflects separate primary tumors presumed to be of different clones. Once
    classed entirely as unfavorable histology, nodular tumors are now divided
    into favorable and unfavorable subsets by applying the neuroblastoma
    criteria of age at diagnosis, grade of neuroblastic differentiation and
    mitosis-karyorrhexis index to the nodules. The nodular component is
    favorable when poorly differentiated or differentiating with a low or
    intermediate index in a patient younger than 18 months, or differentiating
    with a low index from 18 months to 5 years; it is unfavorable when
    undifferentiated or of high index at any age, poorly differentiated or of
    intermediate index from 18 months to 5 years, and of any grade or index from
    5 years (60 months). Every nodular tumor diagnosed at 5 years or older is
    therefore in the unfavorable subset; in a CCG/COG series the
    favorable-subset patients were 4 months to 4 years 11 months old at
    diagnosis, more than half of them younger than 18 months. Umehara and
    colleagues defined the two subsets in 2000 from a review of 70 Children's
    Cancer Group patients. The International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee
    re-reviewed the same patients, confirmed the diagnosis in 67 and the
    survival difference (8-year event-free survival 86.1% in the favorable
    versus 32.2% in the unfavorable subset), and in 2003 proposed adding the
    split to the classification. An INRG analysis identified 232 patients with
    nodular ganglioneuroblastoma among 4,071 with a known INPC category; 83%
    were older than 18 months, 83% had unfavorable INPC histology and 2% had
    MYCN-amplified tumors. Outcome in that cohort was very heterogeneous, with
    event-free and overall survival of 54% and 68%, and in the unfavorable
    subset of a CCG/COG series 5-year event-free survival was 80.1% without and
    16.7% with stage 4 disease. MONDO subdivides the class by background into
    MONDO:0004408 (stroma-rich) and MONDO:0004407 (stroma-dominant).
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:10421273
    reference_title: "The International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification (the Shimada system)."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Approximately 99% of cases (224 of 227) had consensus diagnoses for categorization: neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), 190 cases; ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich), 5 cases; ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant) maturing, 1 case; ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite Schwannian stroma-rich/stroma-dominant and stroma-poor), 19 cases; and NT-unclassifiable, 9 cases."
    explanation: >-
      In a review of 227 neuroblastic tumours by the six-member International
      Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee, consensus diagnoses (five or six of six
      pathologists agreeing) placed 19 cases in ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular
      (composite Schwannian stroma-rich/stroma-dominant and stroma-poor) and 5
      in ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich), as separate
      categories, supporting this subtype as distinct from the intermixed form.
      The counts describe this review set, not subtype frequency. The abstract
      gives no morphologic criteria (described separately) and presents the
      classification as a modified Shimada classification, so it does not show
      that the split originated here.
  - reference: PMID:10964346

  - reference: PMID:14601099
    reference_title: "Revision of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification: confirmation of favorable and unfavorable prognostic subsets in ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular."
    supports: SUPPORT

    explanation: >-
      The International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee reviewed 70 tumours
      diagnosed as nodular ganglioneuroblastoma in CCG studies 3881 and 3891,
      confirmed the diagnosis in 67, and applied neuroblastoma's age-linked
      histology criteria to their neuroblastomatous component, giving favourable
      (22) and unfavourable (45) subsets. Their 8-year event-free survival
      differed significantly (86.1% vs 32.2%), and the committee proposed
      modifying the classification to separate them. These patients had been
      used in a previous study: Umehara 2000 (PMID:10964346) reviewed 70
      patients from the same two studies, and Okamatsu 2009 (PMID:19530234)
      includes them, so this is not independent confirmation.

  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "The proportion of neuroblastomatous nodule(s) to Ganglioneuroblastoma-intermixed/Ganglioneuroma area varies from case to case"
    explanation: >-
      Table I of this CCG/COG clinicopathological series restates the INPC
      definitions; this sentence, from its ganglioneuroblastoma-nodular row,
      says the proportion of neuroblastomatous nodule to intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma tissue varies from case to case.
      The row sets no stroma threshold, whereas the intermixed row requires the
      ganglioneuromatous component to exceed 50% of the total volume in
      representative microscopic fields, so the over-half criterion that MONDO
      and NCIT apply to the whole class corresponds to the INPC intermixed
      criterion. It is a classification rule restated from the INPC papers, not
      a result of this cohort.

      the strength of the metastatic neuroblastoma. The series includes the 70

pathophysiology:
- name: Neoplastic Neuroblast Population
  description: >-
    The malignant neuroblastic cells of the tumor, which form the microscopic
    neuroblastic nests of intermixed tumors and the nodules of nodular tumors.
    In a 1996 series of 20 maturing and mature neuroblastomas, the neuroblastic
    and ganglionic cells carried numerical chromosome aberrations, with
    near-triploidy in 18 of 19 tumors. Flow cytometry nonetheless reads most
    GN-maturing and intermixed tumors as diploid (160 of 191 in a CCG/COG
    series), which the authors suggest may reflect the predominance of
    Schwannian stromal cells in the samples.
  biological_scale: CELLULAR
  cell_types:
  - preferred_term: neoplastic neuroblast
    term:
      id: CL:0002676
      label: neural crest derived neuroblast
  notes: >-
    No node is marked as the origin node (genetic_context), because the cited
    sources describe no initiating lesion for ganglioneuroblastoma.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:8618605
    reference_title: "Role of ploidy, chromosome 1p, and Schwann cells in the maturation of neuroblastoma."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Neuroblastic and ganglionic cells showed aberrations in the number of chromosomes. In situ hybridization and flow cytometry demonstrated near-trip-loidy in 18 of 19 tumors and pentaploidy in the remaining tumor."
    explanation: >-
      In 20 human maturing and mature neuroblastomas (maturing neuroblastoma
      being the abstract's term for a tumour that differentiates toward
      ganglioneuroma), in situ hybridization and flow cytometry found abnormal
      chromosome numbers in the neuroblastic and ganglionic cells:
      near-triploidy in 18 of 19 tumours and pentaploidy in the remaining one.
      The 1996 study predates the International Neuroblastoma Pathology
      Classification and does not say how many cases would now be classed as
      ganglioneuroblastoma. Reading these aneuploid cells as tumour cells is an
      inference from the finding.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "In our series, the majority of GN-M and GNB-I tumors, even though they were biologically favorable with an excellent prognosis, had a diploid pattern determined by flow cytometry. This could be attributable to the fact that those tumors were mainly or predominantly composed of Schwannian stromal cells, and neuroblastic cells were not the main source of cells to be tested."
    explanation: >-
      In this CCG/COG series of 552 centrally reviewed ganglioneuroma and
      ganglioneuroblastoma tumours, most GN-maturing (a ganglioneuroma subtype)
      and GNB-intermixed tumours had a diploid pattern by flow cytometry despite
      being biologically favourable; ploidy data came from COG cases only. The
      authors suggest, hedging with 'could be attributable', that these tumours
      are mainly Schwannian stroma, so neuroblastic cells were not the main
      cells tested. The paper reports no ploidy on separated cell populations,
      so this is a possible explanation, not evidence of which cells, if any,
      are hyperdiploid.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The majority (160/191, 84%) of GN-M and GNB-I tumors had a diploid pattern determined by flow cytometry."
    explanation: >-
      The abstract of this CCG/COG series quantifies the diploid majority: 160
      of the 191 GN-maturing and GNB-intermixed tumours with flow-cytometric
      ploidy data, all from COG cases, had a diploid pattern. The paper's Table
      III splits this into 28 of 32 GN-maturing (a ganglioneuroma subtype) and
      132 of 159 GNB-intermixed tumours. The sentence does not say why these
      tumours read diploid, and it does not cover nodular tumours, whose DNA
      indices the paper reports as split almost evenly between diploid and
      hyperdiploid.
  downstream:
  - target: Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation
    causal_link_type: DIRECT
    description: >-
      Neoplastic neuroblasts mature into ganglion cells. In nodular tumors,
      ganglion cells carry the MYCN gain found in the neuroblasts, while Schwann
      cells do not.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:25081755
      reference_title: "The neuroblastoma and ganglion components of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma are genetically similar: evidence against separate clonal origins."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "By in situ hybridization, MYCN gain (4-10 gene copies/nucleus) was detected in 7/8 neuroblastoma samples. In ganglioneuromatous regions, gains were also detected in ganglion cells but not in Schwann cells."
      explanation: >-
        In paraffin-embedded tissue from eight nodular ganglioneuroblastomas, in
        situ hybridization detected MYCN gain in 7 of 8 neuroblastoma samples
        and, in ganglioneuromatous regions, also in ganglion cells but not in
        Schwann cells. Sharing the neuroblasts' alteration places the ganglion
        cells in the tumour lineage; that they arise by maturation of the
        neuroblasts, rather than from a common precursor, is an inference the
        abstract does not state. The series is small and limited to nodular
        tumours, and the abstract does not say how many had the gain in ganglion
        cells.
  - target: Schwannian Stromal Development
    causal_link_type: INDIRECT_UNKNOWN_INTERMEDIATES
    description: >-
      In co-culture, neuroblastoma tumors and cell lines stimulate Schwann cell
      proliferation, which the authors read as a hint that neoplastic
      neuroblasts drive stromal growth in tumors. The evidence is in vitro and
      says nothing about where the stromal cells come from.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:11464875
      reference_title: "Neuroblastoma cells provoke Schwann cell proliferation in vitro."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: IN_VITRO
      snippet: "Human NB tumours or cell lines unequivocally increased the proliferation of SCs in vitro. ... The results of the cocultivation experiments provide an experimental hint that the in vivo growth of SCs in NBs is caused by the neoplastic neuroblasts"
      explanation: >-
        In co-culture, human neuroblastoma tumours and cell lines increased the
        proliferation of human Schwann cells, and the authors conclude that this
        is an experimental hint that Schwann cell growth within neuroblastomas
        in vivo is caused by the neoplastic neuroblasts. The support for this
        edge is indirect: the stimulation was shown in vitro, so the in vivo
        link is the authors' inference rather than an observation in patients'
        tumours, and no co-cultured tumour is identified as
        ganglioneuroblastoma. The result concerns Schwann cell proliferation,
        not where the stromal cells come from.
  - target: Schwannian Stroma-Poor Neuroblastomatous Nodule
    causal_link_type: INDIRECT_UNKNOWN_INTERMEDIATES
    description: >-
      In 7 of 8 nodular tumors studied, the neuroblasts of the nodule carried
      MYCN copy gain, consistent with the nodule being formed by the neoplastic
      neuroblast population.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:25081755
      reference_title: "The neuroblastoma and ganglion components of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma are genetically similar: evidence against separate clonal origins."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "By in situ hybridization, MYCN gain (4-10 gene copies/nucleus) was detected in 7/8 neuroblastoma samples."
      explanation: >-
        A case series of eight nodular ganglioneuroblastomas found, by in situ
        hybridization, MYCN gain (4-10 gene copies per nucleus) in the
        neuroblastoma component, which forms the nodule, in 7 of 8 tumours.
        Finding this copy-number change in the nodule's neuroblasts supports the
        inference that the nodule is formed by the neoplastic neuroblast
        population. The abstract reports this as MYCN gain and does not describe
        it as amplification. The result does not show whether the nodule is a
        separate clone.
  - target: Abdominal Mass
    causal_link_type: DIRECT

        ganglioneuroblastoma. A primary tumor at either site is an abdominal
        mass, but that step is inferred: the definition names sites, not a mass,

        therefore supported indirectly.

        (74%). Reading an abdominal primary as an abdominal mass is an
        inference, and the figure covers one subtype at a referral center.

- name: Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation
  description: >-
    Neoplastic neuroblasts differentiate into ganglion cells. Biologically
    favorable peripheral neuroblastic tumors are considered to mature by
    age-dependent steps from neuroblastoma through intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma to ganglioneuroma.

    explanation: >-
      This 2026 pathology review states that biologically favourable peripheral
      neuroblastic tumours are considered to proceed by age-dependent
      differentiation and maturation steps from neuroblastoma to GNB-intermixed
      and finally to ganglioneuroma, the maturation this node describes. The
      sentence sits in the review's account of the INPC categories and gives a
      consensus view ('are considered to') without supporting data. It covers
      only the favourable path through GNB-intermixed and says nothing about how
      nodular tumours arise.
- name: Schwannian Stromal Development
  description: >-
    The Schwann-cell stroma that makes up more than half of an intermixed tumor
    and forms the background of a nodular one. The INPC names each category by
    its Schwannian stromal development, which gives intermixed tumors their
    elastic, yellowish-white gross appearance. In culture, neuroblastoma tumors
    and cell lines stimulate Schwann cell proliferation, and Schwann cell
    products induce neuronal differentiation of neuroblastoma cells. Whether the
    stromal cells are non-neoplastic cells recruited by the tumor or derive from
    the neoplastic clone is contested (discussion gnb_schwannian_stroma_origin).
  biological_scale: TISSUE
  cell_types:
  - preferred_term: Schwannian stromal cell
    term:
      id: CL:0002573
      label: Schwann cell
  biological_processes:
  - preferred_term: Schwann cell proliferation
    term:
      id: GO:0014010
      label: Schwann cell proliferation
  notes: >-
    The node is named for the INPC term Schwannian stromal development rather
    than for stroma recruitment, because recruitment presumes the non-neoplastic
    origin that is still disputed.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Tumors in this category are elastic in consistency and yellowish white in color due to Schwannian stromal development (Fig. 2A)."
    explanation: >-
      In this 2026 pathology review the sentence opens the paragraph on
      ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed; it cites Fig. 2A, which shows the gross
      appearance of an adrenal intermixed tumour with a solid, elastic cut
      surface, and the next sentence defines the category as more than 50%
      ganglioneuromatous tissue. It attributes the elastic, yellowish-white
      gross appearance of these tumours to their Schwannian stromal development.
      This is the authors' descriptive synthesis rather than a measured cohort
      result, and the review calls ganglioneuroma elastic and yellowish white
      too.
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "INPC defines 4 categories in the pNTs: Neuroblastoma (NB; Schwannian stroma-poor), Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (GNB-i; Schwannian stroma-rich), Ganglioneuroma (GN; Schwannian stroma-dominant), and Ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (GNB-n; composite, Schwannian stroma-rich/stroma-dominant and stroma-poor) ... In order to clarify their histologic characteristics, each category is often followed by its unique feature of Schwannian stromal development in parenthesis."
    explanation: >-
      This 2026 pathology review restates the four INPC categories with their
      Schwannian stromal features; ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed is
      stroma-rich, and the nodular form is composite, combining stroma-rich or
      stroma-dominant with stroma-poor tissue. The next sentence, after an
      omitted citation number, says each parenthetical gives the category's
      unique feature of Schwannian stromal development. This supports stromal
      development as a defining feature of both ganglioneuroblastoma subtypes.
      It restates a classification rule rather than new data and does not
      address gross appearance.
  downstream:
  - target: Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation
    causal_link_type: INDIRECT_UNKNOWN_INTERMEDIATES
    description: >-
      Soluble Schwann cell products induce neuronal differentiation of
      neuroblastoma cells. The evidence is from cultured neuroblastoma cells,
      not from ganglioneuroblastoma tissue.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:9788610
      reference_title: "Schwann cell-conditioned medium promotes neuroblastoma survival and differentiation."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: IN_VITRO
      snippet: "Schwann cell-conditioned medium induced extensive neurite outgrowth in all of the neuroblastoma cell lines, and these processes contained mature neurofilament in three of the cell lines."
      explanation: >-
        Four human neuroblastoma cell lines were cultured for 7 days in medium
        conditioned by human Schwann cells, which carries their secreted
        products but no Schwann cells. All four lines grew extensive neurites,
        and in three the processes contained mature neurofilament, so soluble
        Schwann cell products induced neuritic differentiation, supporting this
        edge. The abstract does not name the active factors, and the model is
        cell lines rather than ganglioneuroblastoma tissue, so the support is
        indirect.
    - reference: PMID:33712610
      reference_title: "Schwann cell plasticity regulates neuroblastic tumor cell differentiation via epidermal growth factor-like protein 8."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: IN_VITRO
      snippet: "Neuroblastoma cells, derived from aggressive tumors, respond to primary repair-related SCs and their secretome with increased neuronal differentiation and reduced proliferation."
      explanation: >-
        Three neuroblastoma cell lines and two patient-derived short-term
        cultures were grown with primary human Schwann cells from excised
        peripheral nerve, either in contact or on a trans-well insert that let
        soluble molecules diffuse without contact. Without contact the neuronal
        differentiation marker NF200 rose significantly by day 16 in four of the
        five cultures, which places the effect on released Schwann cell factors
        rather than cell contact. The experiments are in vitro, the no-contact
        readout was NF200 rather than neurite length, and no
        ganglioneuroblastoma tissue was studied.
    - reference: PMID:11792807
      reference_title: "Pigment epithelium-derived factor (PEDF) in neuroblastoma: a multifunctional mediator of Schwann cell antitumor activity."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: IN_VITRO
      snippet: "PEDF was the major factor responsible for Schwann cell's ability to induce tumor cell differentiation in vitro"
      explanation: >-
        In culture, pigment epithelium-derived factor (PEDF) was the major
        factor behind Schwann cells' ability to induce differentiation of
        neuroblastoma tumor cells. The abstract also reports that PEDF is
        produced by ganglionic cells and Schwann cells but not by more primitive
        tumor cells, so it names one Schwann cell product behind this edge. The
        support is indirect: the result is in vitro, and the abstract does not
        say which Schwann cells or tumor cells were used or whether any tumor
        studied was a ganglioneuroblastoma. 'Major' does not mean sole;
        PMID:33712610 identifies another secreted Schwann cell protein, EGFL8,
        as a neuritogen.
- name: Schwannian Stroma-Poor Neuroblastomatous Nodule
  subtypes:
  - Nodular
  description: >-
    One or more macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic, Schwannian stroma-poor nodules
    of neuroblastoma within intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma
    tissue. The nodular components are not always aggressive, and the age-linked
    histopathology of the nodules predicts prognosis. Whether the nodule is a
    clone distinct from the rest of the tumor is contested (discussion
    gnb_nodule_clonality).
  biological_scale: TISSUE
  cell_types:
  - preferred_term: neoplastic neuroblast
    term:
      id: CL:0002676
      label: neural crest derived neuroblast
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:10964346
    reference_title: "Histopathology defines prognostic subsets of ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The current study demonstrates that the nodular components in GNBn tumors are not always aggressive. The prognosis of these patients can be determined by the analysis of age-linked histopathologic features."
    explanation: >-
      Conclusion of a review of pathology and outcomes for 70 patients with
      nodular ganglioneuroblastoma in the CCG-3881 and CCG-3891 studies. It
      states that the nodular components are not always aggressive and that
      prognosis can be determined from age-linked histopathology, defined in the
      Methods as the INPC neuroblastoma criteria (age, differentiation grade,
      mitosis-karyorrhexis index) applied to the nodules. The abstract does not
      separate stage from histology: 63.0% of unfavourable-subset patients
      presented with distant metastasis. PMID:14601099 re-reviewed the same
      patients and PMID:19530234 includes them, so these papers are not
      independent confirmation.
  downstream:
  - target: Distant Metastasis at Diagnosis
    causal_link_type: DIRECT
    description: >-
      In the unfavorable subset, distant metastasis from the neuroblastomatous
      nodule is often already present at diagnosis, even when the primary tumor
      is completely resected.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:19530234
      reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: DIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "It was also noted that 44.0% (70/159) of GNB-N-US cases with completely resected primary tumor had already established distant metastasis from unfavorable NB nodule at the time of diagnosis."
      explanation: >-
        In a CCG/COG central pathology review of 552 ganglioneuroma and
        ganglioneuroblastoma tumours, the authors state that 70 (44.0%) of 159
        patients in the unfavourable nodular subset (GNB-N-US) with a completely
        resected primary tumour already had distant metastasis from the
        unfavourable neuroblastomatous nodule at diagnosis. It supports this
        edge for the unfavourable subset only. The attribution rests on
        histological findings, and in 6 completely resected cases no
        unfavourable nodule was found in the primary, only at the metastatic
        site. Elsewhere the paper counts 74 of the 159, not 70.
- name: Telomere Maintenance Mechanism Activation
  biological_scale: MOLECULAR
  conforms_to: "enabling_replicative_immortality#Telomere Maintenance Reactivation"
  mechanism_confidence: PROVISIONAL
  description: >-
    A subset of ganglioneuroblastoma maintains its telomeres, by either of the
    two routes used in neuroblastoma: alternative lengthening of telomeres,
    detected as C-circles, or raised TERT expression. In a Japanese series of
    48 ganglioneuroblastomas, alternative lengthening was present in 6 and
    raised TERT expression in 7 of the 31 tested, with 12 of the 48 positive
    by either route. The activation is concentrated in the nodular subtype,
    where 10 of 21 were positive, a proportion close to the neuroblastoma
    figure in the same series. In the 18 ganglioneuromas, alternative
    lengthening was absent and one tumour had raised TERT expression. Among
    the positive ganglioneuroblastomas the authors report frequent ATRX
    alteration and infrequent TERT rearrangement, which they take to mean
    that TERT-high ganglioneuroblastomas raise TERT expression by some
    mechanism other than the rearrangement and promoter mutation that
    predominate in TERT-high MYCN-not-amplified neuroblastoma. Which
    mechanism that is, the series does not establish.
  genes:
  - preferred_term: TERT
    term:
      id: hgnc:11730
      label: TERT
  - preferred_term: ATRX
    term:
      id: hgnc:886
      label: ATRX
  biological_processes:
  - preferred_term: telomere maintenance via telomerase
    modifier: INCREASED
    term:
      id: GO:0007004
      label: telomere maintenance via telomerase
  - preferred_term: telomere maintenance via recombination
    modifier: INCREASED
    term:
      id: GO:0000722
      label: telomere maintenance via recombination
  notes: >-
    The node binds both routes rather than splitting them, because the one
    series that measured them in ganglioneuroblastoma reports both and
    defines its positive group as either. Neuroblastoma models them as
    separate nodes, which that entry's larger evidence base supports; this
    entry should split them when a second series makes the two separately
    characterisable here.
    No causal edge is drawn to or from the nodular subtype node. The
    relationship reported is enrichment within a histological subtype, not
    one mechanism producing the other, and the subtype is already carried
    structurally in has_subtypes. The node is marked PROVISIONAL because the
    whole ganglioneuroblastoma claim rests on one cohort of 48, and because
    the series was assembled in Japan from tumours resected between 2014 and
    2018 in children under 10, so neither the proportions nor their absence
    in ganglioneuroma should be read as population estimates.
    The same paper's chromosomal-aberration findings are deliberately not
    cited here; arm-level aberration evidence is curated in the neuroblastoma
    entry, not this one.
    No cell type is bound. The C-circle and TERT assays were run on bulk
    tumour nucleic acid, and a ganglioneuroblastoma is by definition a mixture
    of neuroblastic, ganglionic and Schwannian stromal components, so a bulk
    measurement cannot say which of them carries the telomere-maintenance
    activity. Attributing it to the neoplastic neuroblast would assert a
    cell-type localisation the assay does not resolve, and in this entry that
    is the very question the Schwannian-stroma-origin discussion records as
    unsettled. Whether the activity sits in the neuroblastic component is a
    separate claim needing a cell-resolved measurement.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41858373
    reference_title: "Telomere maintenance mechanisms are activated in ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "ALT was identified in 38 NB cases (38/255, 15%) and 6 GNB cases (6/48, 12.5%), but not in GN. High TERT expression was observed in 38% (64/169), 23% (7/31) and 14% (1/7) of NB, GNB and GN cases, respectively."
    explanation: >-
      The measurement behind the node: both telomere-maintenance routes
      assayed in ganglioneuroblastoma tumours, with the denominators stated.
      Note the TERT denominators are smaller than the ALT ones, 31 of 48
      ganglioneuroblastomas and 7 of 18 ganglioneuromas, so the two routes
      were not tested in the same number of tumours and the absence of
      alternative lengthening in ganglioneuroma rests on 18 cases.
  - reference: PMID:41858373
    reference_title: "Telomere maintenance mechanisms are activated in ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "TMM activation, defined as ALT(+) and/or high TERT expression, occurred in 12/48 GNB cases and 1/18 GN cases, particularly in the GNB-nodular type (10/21, 48%), which was similar to 39% (100/255) of NB cases."
    explanation: >-
      The combined figure and the subtype concentration, which is what makes
      this node more than a curiosity: nearly half of nodular
      ganglioneuroblastomas are positive, close to the neuroblastoma
      proportion in the same cohort. The definition is the authors' own and
      is a disjunction, so a positive tumour need not have both routes. The
      comparison with neuroblastoma is within this one series.
  - reference: PMID:41858373
    reference_title: "Telomere maintenance mechanisms are activated in ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Furthermore, TMM(+) GNBs exhibited distinct features, including a high frequency of ATRX alterations and a lower frequency of TERT rearrangements."
    explanation: >-
      Why ATRX is bound on this node alongside TERT. Indirect for the node's
      own claim: the sentence describes what distinguishes the positive
      tumours rather than measuring telomere maintenance, and it reports a
      frequency contrast without giving the counts behind it.
  - reference: PMID:41858373
    reference_title: "Telomere maintenance mechanisms are activated in ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "mutations or structural abnormalities in the TERT promoter region are the main mechanisms of TERT-high MYCN-not amplified NBs, whereas they are less frequent in TERT-high GNBs. This suggests that GNB utilizes other mechanisms to achieve TERT-high phenotype."
    explanation: >-
      The authors' own reading of the low TERT-rearrangement frequency, and the
      reason the description now states it rather than an inference drawn from
      it. The claim is about the TERT-high ganglioneuroblastomas specifically:
      whatever raises TERT in them is not the rearrangement or promoter
      mutation that does so in MYCN-not-amplified neuroblastoma. It is not a
      claim that the recombination route predominates in ganglioneuroblastoma
      overall, and the series' own counts would not support that: alternative
      lengthening in 6 of 48 against raised TERT in 7 of 31 tested are
      comparable rates. The replacement mechanism is left unnamed because the
      paper does not identify it.
histopathology:
- name: Schwannian Stroma-Rich Ganglioneuromatous Component With Microscopic Neuroblastic Nests
  finding_term:
    preferred_term: ganglioneuromatous (Schwannian stroma-rich) component over 50% of the tumor with residual microscopic neuroblastic nests
    term:
      id: NCIT:C42062
      label: Schwannian Stroma More than 50 Percent of the Tumor Volume
  description: >-
    A ganglioneuromatous, Schwannian stroma-rich component that exceeds 50% of
    the tumor in representative sections, with scattered residual microscopic
    neuroblastomatous foci: pockets of neuropil containing neuroblasts,
    differentiating neuroblasts and ganglion cells. The tumor is maturing, but
    the process is incomplete. Under the INPC the favorable or unfavorable
    histology group is to be determined only from primary or metastatic tumor
    tissue obtained before chemotherapy or irradiation, and once assigned it is
    not to be changed during the clinical course, because therapy-associated
    changes, including neuroblastic differentiation and even Schwannian stromal
    development, may not have proven prognostic significance. A diagnosis of
    intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma made on a tumor resected after neoadjuvant
    chemotherapy does not take the effect of that chemotherapy into account.
  diagnostic: true
  context: Primary or metastatic tumor tissue obtained before chemotherapy or irradiation, classified by the INPC
  subtype: Intermixed
  notes: >-
    NCIT:C42057 Ganglioneuroblastoma, Intermixed names the neoplasm rather than
    a microscopic finding, so the finding is bound to the morphologic-finding
    term NCIT:C42062.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Ganglioneuroblastoma-Intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich): A transitional form between Neuroblastoma and Ganglioneuroma. Tumor is on its way toward full differentiation/maturation; however, the process is not complete, as evidenced by scattered \"residual\" microscopic neuroblastomatous foci presenting as pockets of neuropil containing varying numbers of neuroblastic cells with various stages of maturation including neuroblasts, differentiating neuroblasts, and/or ganglion cells. The proportion of the ganglioneuromatous component to \"residual\" neuroblastomatous foci should exceed 50% of the total volume in microscopic field(s) from representative section(s) of the tumor."
    explanation: >-
      Table I of this CCG/COG clinicopathological study of 552 ganglioneuroma
      and ganglioneuroblastoma cases restates the International Neuroblastoma
      Pathology Classification definition of the intermixed category. The
      definition gives the histology this finding records: a transitional form
      between neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma whose maturation is incomplete,
      with scattered 'residual' microscopic neuroblastomatous foci (pockets of
      neuropil holding neuroblastic cells) and a ganglioneuromatous component
      exceeding 50% of the total volume. It is a diagnostic criterion, not
      cohort data, and the 50% threshold applies to microscopic fields from
      representative sections, not to the whole tumour volume.
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "The distinction of FH group or UH group according to the INPC, as summarized in Table 1, should only be determined based on the pathology review of primary or metastatic tumors obtained before starting chemotherapy/irradiation therapy. Once FH or UH group classification is assigned, it should not be changed throughout the clinical course of individual cases, since therapy-associated histology changes including neuroblastic cytodifferentiation and even Schwannian stromal development, if present, may not have proven prognostic significance"
    explanation: >-
      This 2026 pathology review states the INPC rule immediately after noting
      that the neuroblastoma nodules of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma are graded
      by age-linked criteria and that intermixed tumors are always of favorable
      histology. The favorable or unfavorable histology group is to be
      determined only from pathology review of primary or metastatic tumors
      obtained before chemotherapy or irradiation starts and, once assigned, is
      not to be changed during the clinical course, because therapy-associated
      changes such as neuroblastic cytodifferentiation and even Schwannian
      stromal development may not have proven prognostic significance. The
      passage states a classification rule and reports no patient data; it
      concerns the histology group, not how a tumor first sampled after
      treatment should be categorized.
  - reference: PMID:37243318
    reference_title: "Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed: Clinicopathological implications of diagnosis at presentation and genomic correlations."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Diagnosing GNBI on morphological examination of the definitively resected tumor does not take into account the impact of neoadjuvant chemotherapy that might be administered before definitive tumor resection."
    explanation: >-
      In the Discussion of their Memorial Sloan Kettering series, the authors
      note that a diagnosis of intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) made by
      examining the definitively resected tumor does not take into account the
      effect of any neoadjuvant chemotherapy given before that resection. It is
      a framing statement, not a result of the series, and does not say how
      often or in what way chemotherapy changes the histology.
- name: Macroscopic Neuroblastomatous Nodule in a Stroma-Rich or Stroma-Dominant Background

    A primary tumor of the adrenal medulla or extra-adrenal retroperitoneum
    forms an abdominal mass. In one referral series of intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma, 74% of primary tumors were abdominal.
  phenotype_term:

      id: HP:0031500
      label: Abdominal mass
  evidence:
  - reference: ORPHA:251877
    reference_title: "Ganglioneuroblastoma"
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "usually occurring in the posterior mediastinum, adrenal medulla and extra-adrenal retroperitoneum (but sometimes in the neck and pelvis)"
    explanation: >-
      Orphanet's disease definition, which reports no patient data, names the
      posterior mediastinum, adrenal medulla and extra-adrenal retroperitoneum
      as the usual sites of ganglioneuroblastoma, and the neck and pelvis as
      occasional ones. A primary tumor in the adrenal medulla or retroperitoneum
      is an abdominal mass, but that step is inferred: the definition names
      sites, not a mass, and its list of clinical signs does not include one. It
      does not rank the sites and lists the posterior mediastinum first, so it
      does not show that abdominal primaries predominate.
  - reference: PMID:37243318
    reference_title: "Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed: Clinicopathological implications of diagnosis at presentation and genomic correlations."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The primary tumor was abdominal in 39 (74%) and thoracic in 14 patients (26%)."
    explanation: >-
      Retrospective Memorial Sloan Kettering series of 53 patients whose
      intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma was diagnosed at presentation. The primary
      tumor was abdominal in 39 (74%) and thoracic in 14 (26%), so most of these
      tumors arose in the abdomen. Reading an abdominal primary as an abdominal
      mass is an inference, and the paper does not name the abdominal sites. The
      figure covers one subtype at a tertiary referral center, where only 16 of
      the 53 patients were first diagnosed; it is not an estimate for
      ganglioneuroblastoma as a whole.

      nodular ganglioneuroblastoma (61.6%). The p-values compare ganglioneuroma

      ones (p2); none compares intermixed tumors with neuroblastoma. The
      abstract's finding that mature tumors less often had elevated metabolites
      comes from the pooled column, three quarters of which is ganglioneuroma.
      Metastatic tumors were excluded, and nodular tumors are not reported apart
      from neuroblastoma.

  frequency: VERY_RARE
  notes: >-
    MYCN amplification is rare in ganglioneuroblastoma. In a Children's Cancer
    Group series it was found in none of 21 intermixed and 2 of 63 nodular
    tumors. In a CCG/COG series of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma, 10
    of 500 patients with a MYCN result had amplified tumors: 2 GN-maturing and 2
    intermixed tumors, all alive and well at last follow-up, and 6 nodular
    tumors of the unfavorable subset. These cooperative-group series overlap.
    MYCN status is used with INPC histology to assign neuroblastic tumors to
    risk groups. A single-center series (PMID:28465480) reported amplification
    in 14% of nodular and none of the intermixed tumors; its Results record a
    single amplified nodular tumor, and the authors note that only a small
    number of patients were tested.

diagnosis:
- name: Histopathologic Classification
  description: >-
    Ganglioneuroblastoma is diagnosed and subtyped by histopathologic
    examination of tumor tissue under the INPC. Because a macroscopic
    neuroblastomatous nodule separates nodular from intermixed tumors, which are
    hard to tell apart on limited samples, the completely resected tumor is the
    ideal sample. When only a biopsy or an incompletely resected tumor is
    available, a favorable classification is reported as based on review of
    limited material: the CCG/COG central reviewers did so, and a 2026 pathology
    review advises it for core needle biopsies that show only intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma.
  diagnosis_term:
    preferred_term: histopathologic examination under the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification
    term:
      id: NCIT:C18190
      label: Histopathologic Examination
  evidence:

    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "The diagnosis of GNBI is typically made on a diligent morphological examination of the entire resected tumor because distinguishing GNBI from GNBN is particularly challenging on limited samples."
    explanation: >-
      The Discussion of this Memorial Sloan Kettering series opens by restating
      established practice: intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) is usually
      diagnosed on careful examination of the entire resected tumor, because it
      is particularly hard to tell from nodular ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBN) on
      limited samples. It states practice and reports no data from the series.

differential_diagnoses:
- name: Ganglioneuroma
  description: >-
    Ganglioneuroma is the Schwannian stroma-dominant category of the peripheral
    neuroblastic tumors, and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is the Schwannian
    stroma-rich category beside it. Both consist largely of Schwannian stroma
    containing mature or maturing ganglion cells, and the INPC maturing subtype
    of ganglioneuroma may also hold a minor component of individually scattered
    differentiating neuroblasts. Read side by side, their INPC definitions
    differ in the well-defined microscopic neuroblastic nests that characterize
    the intermixed category; differentiating neuroblasts scattered as single
    cells are compatible with maturing ganglioneuroma. For decades
    ganglioneuroblastoma caused some consternation for pathologists and
    oncologists, one reason being that there was no clear and objective
    delineation between it and ganglioneuroma. The INPC maturing subtype of
    ganglioneuroma is the tumor group that the earlier Shimada classification
    called ganglioneuroblastoma, well differentiated, so a tumor reported as
    well-differentiated ganglioneuroblastoma under the Shimada nomenclature is
    a ganglioneuroma under the INPC. Sampling adds another source of confusion: compared with
    surgical resection, core needle biopsy seems to be at a disadvantage with
    nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, in which a neuroblastoma nodule coexists with
    intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma tissue.
  disease_term:
    preferred_term: ganglioneuroma
    term:
      id: MONDO:0005033
      label: ganglioneuroma
  distinguishing_features:
  - "Intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is defined as tumor tissue more than 50% occupied by a ganglioneuromatous component and is characterized by well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells whose neurites are segmentally naked within the nests."
  - "In ganglioneuroma, ganglion cells lie individually or in small clusters in Schwannian stroma and are either all completely mature (mature subtype) or a mixture of mature and maturing cells (maturing subtype); completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells, and naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells."
  - "Under the INPC definition the maturing subtype of ganglioneuroma may also contain a minor component of individually scattered differentiating neuroblasts, present as single scattered cells rather than in the microscopic neuroblastic nests that characterize intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma."
  - "Under the INPC, intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is Schwannian stroma-rich and ganglioneuroma Schwannian stroma-dominant, while nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is a composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue."
  - "Compared with surgical resection, core needle biopsy seems to be at a disadvantage with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, where a neuroblastoma nodule coexists with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma tissue; when the tumor is large, multiple cores may give a better chance than incisional biopsy of sampling the nodule, which is often hemorrhagic or necrotic and detected on radiological images."
  notes: >-
    MONDO:0005033 also has central nervous system parents (MONDO:0000640 central
    nervous system primitive neuroectodermal neoplasm and MONDO:0016729 mixed
    neuronal-glial tumor); this row concerns the neuroblastic tumor of the
    sympathetic nervous system that the MONDO definition describes.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "By definition, more than 50% of the tumor tissue in this category is occupied by a ganglioneuromatous component (see below), where well-developed Schwannian stroma contains sporadic mature or maturing ganglion cells. The tumors are characterized by the presence of well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells in various stages of differentiation with actively producing and easily recognizable neurites around their cytoplasm (Fig. 2B). Their neurites are segmentally naked in those microscopic nests and eventually incorporated in the cytoplasm of Schwannian cells in the stromal areas. ... Tumor cells (ganglion cells) are distributed individually or making small clusters in Schwannian stroma (Fig. 2C). They are either completely mature (mature GN) or a mixture of mature and maturing ganglion cells (maturing GN). Completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells. Neurites produced by the ganglion cells are immediately incorporated in the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, so that naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells."
    explanation: >-
      In this 2026 pathology review, part one of the quote comes from the
      section headed 'Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich)'
      and says only 'this category'; the Fig. 2B legend it cites labels the
      image as ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed. Part two comes from the section
      headed 'Ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant)' and names its
      category through the mature and maturing GN subtypes. Restating the
      International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification, the review defines
      intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma as tumor tissue more than 50% occupied by
      a ganglioneuromatous component and characterizes it by well-defined
      microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells whose neurites are segmentally
      naked within the nests. In ganglioneuroma, ganglion cells lie individually
      or in small clusters in Schwannian stroma and are either completely mature
      (mature GN) or a mixture of mature and maturing cells (maturing GN);
      completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells, and
      because their neurites are immediately incorporated into Schwannian
      stromal cells, naked neurites are not identified around them. The review
      describes the two categories in separate paragraphs and does not compare
      them directly; read side by side, the definitions set the neuroblastic
      nests of the intermixed tumor against the individually distributed or
      clustered ganglion cells of ganglioneuroma. These are classification
      criteria, not case data.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Ganglioneuroma-Maturing (Schwannian stroma-dominant): Composed predominantly of ganglioneuromatous stroma with a minor component of individually scattered, evenly or unevenly distributed differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells, in addition to fully mature ganglion cells.     explanation: >-
      Table I of this CCG/COG clinicopathological series restates the
      International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification definition of the
      ganglioneuroma, maturing subtype (Schwannian stroma-dominant): mostly
      ganglioneuromatous stroma with a minor component of individually
      scattered, evenly or unevenly distributed differentiating neuroblasts
      and/or maturing ganglion cells, beside fully mature ganglion cells that
      are always covered with satellite cells. So differentiating neuroblasts
      scattered as single cells can occur in this ganglioneuroma subtype. The
      intermixed row of the same table instead describes scattered
      'residual' microscopic neuroblastomatous foci presenting as pockets of
      neuropil; the contrast between single scattered cells and those foci is
      read from the two definitions side by side and is not stated in this
      row. It is a classification rule restated from the INPC papers, not a
      result of this cohort.
  - reference: PMID:10421272
    reference_title: "Terminology and morphologic criteria of neuroblastic tumors: recommendations by the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3) ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant), maturing and mature; and 4) ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite Schwannian stroma-richlstroma-dominant and stroma-poor)."
    explanation: >-
      This is the 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma
      Pathology Committee, whose six member pathologists defined morphologically
      based classifications on the basis of a review of 227 cases. It classifies
      neuroblastic tumors into four categories, each labeled by its Schwannian
      stroma: neuroblastoma (stroma-poor), ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed
      (stroma-rich), ganglioneuroma (stroma-dominant, with maturing and mature
      subtypes) and ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular, a composite of stroma-rich or
      stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue. The scheme therefore
      separates ganglioneuroblastoma from stroma-poor neuroblastoma and from
      stroma-dominant ganglioneuroma. The abstract gives each category its name,
      its Schwannian stromal label and, for neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma,
      the names of its subtypes, but not the morphologic criteria that the
      article says it defines. 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant' is the source's
      typo for stroma-rich/stroma-dominant, the spelling in the abstract of the
      companion INPC article in the same issue (PMID:10421273). In this abstract
      INPC abbreviates the Committee, which proposes the International
      Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "For decades, GNB caused some consternation for both pathologists and oncologists for several reasons: Firstly, there were no clear and objective delineations between NB and GNB, and between GNB and GN, and secondly, there were no well-defined guidelines to predict tumor biology and clinical outcome of the patients with GNB. ...
