    explanation: >-
      Two sentences from the introduction of this Children's Cancer Group and
      Children's Oncology Group clinicopathological series of ganglioneuroma and
      ganglioneuroblastoma carry the claim. The first is the authors' account of
      the period before the INPC: for decades ganglioneuroblastoma caused some
      consternation for pathologists and oncologists, firstly because there were
      no clear and objective delineations between neuroblastoma and
      ganglioneuroblastoma, or between ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma.
      The second restates the INPC subdivision of the ganglioneuroma category
      into GN-Maturing and GN-Mature subtypes and equates GN-Maturing with the
      tumor group that the Shimada classification described as
      ganglioneuroblastoma, well differentiated. So the boundary between
      ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma was long unclear, and a tumor
      reported as well-differentiated ganglioneuroblastoma under the earlier
      Shimada nomenclature falls in the INPC ganglioneuroma, maturing subtype,
      not in an INPC ganglioneuroblastoma category. In 'system3' the paper's
      citation number 3 runs into the word. Both sentences are introductory
      framing and restated nomenclature, not results from this cohort.
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Compared with surgical resection, PCNB seems to have disadvantages when dealing with pNTs having intra-tumoral heterogeneity, such as GNB-n where a NB nodule is co-existing with GNB-i or GN. When the given tumor is large in size, however, PCNB with multiple cores may offer a better chance to obtain samples from a NB nodule, that is often hemorrhagic and/or necrotic and detected by radiology images, than incisional biopsy."
    explanation: >-
      This 2026 pathology review states that, compared with surgical resection,
      percutaneous core needle biopsy (PCNB) seems to be at a disadvantage for
      peripheral neuroblastic tumors with intra-tumoral heterogeneity, such as
      nodular ganglioneuroblastoma (GNB-n), where a neuroblastoma (NB) nodule
      coexists with GNB-intermixed (GNB-i) or ganglioneuroma (GN) tissue. When
      the tumor is large, PCNB with multiple cores may give a better chance than
      incisional biopsy of sampling the NB nodule, which is often hemorrhagic or
      necrotic and seen on imaging. So a core showing only GN or GNB-i tissue
      may come from a nodular tumor whose nodule was missed, and the review's
      next sentence advises reporting such a favorable classification as based
      on limited material. It is an expert statement without case data.
- name: Neuroblastoma
  description: >-
    Neuroblastoma is the Schwannian stroma-poor category of the same INPC
    classification, and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is a composite of
    stroma-poor neuroblastoma tissue with stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue,
    so different samples from one patient can be assigned different categories.
    When core needle biopsy samples from one site show intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma and another site in the same patient
    shows neuroblastoma, a 2026 pathology review advises considering a variant
    of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, in which a neuroblastoma nodule in the
    primary tumor was not sampled but is found at the metastatic site, or,
    extremely rarely, multiple primaries presumably composed of different
    clones. In one tertiary-center retrospective series, 12 patients with stage
    M disease had intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma diagnosed on biopsy; after
    neoadjuvant chemotherapy the definitive resection showed neuroblastoma in 8
    (67%), so the biopsy diagnosis could not be corroborated, and the authors
    advise high caution with a biopsy diagnosis of intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma when metastases are present.
  disease_term:
    preferred_term: neuroblastoma
    term:
      id: MONDO:0005072
      label: neuroblastoma
  distinguishing_features:
  - "Histologically, tumors in the INPC neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor) category are composed of neuroblastic cells in sheets or nests demarcated by thin fibrovascular septa, with limited or absent Schwannian stromal development."
  - "Under the INPC, neuroblastoma is Schwannian stroma-poor, intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma Schwannian stroma-rich, and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma a composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue."
  - "Under the INPC definition, intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is a transitional form between neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma with scattered residual microscopic neuroblastomatous foci presenting as pockets of neuropil, and its ganglioneuromatous component should exceed 50% of the total volume in microscopic fields from representative sections of the tumor."
  - "Nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is characterized by macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic neuroblastomatous nodules of Schwannian stroma-poor component coexisting with stroma-rich intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or stroma-dominant ganglioneuroma."
  - "When core needle biopsy samples show intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma at one site and neuroblastoma at another in the same patient, a 2026 pathology review advises considering a variant of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma whose primary-site neuroblastoma nodule was not sampled but is found at the metastatic site or, extremely rarely, multiple primaries presumably composed of different clones."
  - "In one tertiary-center series, resection after neoadjuvant chemotherapy showed neuroblastoma in 8 of 12 patients with stage M disease whose biopsy had shown intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, and the authors advise high caution with such a biopsy diagnosis when metastases are present."
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:10421272
    reference_title: "Terminology and morphologic criteria of neuroblastic tumors: recommendations by the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3) ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant), maturing and mature; and 4) ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite Schwannian stroma-richlstroma-dominant and stroma-poor)."
    explanation: >-
      This is the 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma
      Pathology Committee, whose six member pathologists defined morphologically
      based classifications on the basis of a review of 227 cases. It classifies
      neuroblastic tumors into four categories, each labeled by its Schwannian
      stroma: neuroblastoma (stroma-poor), ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed
      (stroma-rich), ganglioneuroma (stroma-dominant, with maturing and mature
      subtypes) and ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular, a composite of stroma-rich or
      stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue. The scheme therefore
      separates ganglioneuroblastoma from stroma-poor neuroblastoma and from
      stroma-dominant ganglioneuroma. The abstract gives each category its name,
      its Schwannian stromal label and, for neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma,
      the names of its subtypes, but not the morphologic criteria that the
      article says it defines. 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant' is the source's
      typo for stroma-rich/stroma-dominant, the spelling in the abstract of the
      companion INPC article in the same issue (PMID:10421273). In this abstract
      INPC abbreviates the Committee, which proposes the International
      Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification.
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Histologically the tumors are composed of neuroblastic cells arranged in sheets or nests demarcated by thin fibrovascular septae. Schwannian stromal development is limited or absent."
    explanation: >-
      The quoted sentences come from the section of this 2026 review headed
      'Neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor)', whose first sentence names its
      subject as tumors in the NB category, so 'the tumors' are tumors of the
      INPC neuroblastoma category. They consist of neuroblastic cells in sheets
      or nests demarcated by thin fibrovascular septa ('septae' in the source),
      with limited or absent Schwannian stromal development. The same review
      labels intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma Schwannian stroma-rich, so the
      extent of Schwannian stroma, rather than the neuroblastic architecture,
      separates neuroblastoma from the intermixed form; that comparison is drawn
      from the review's definitions and is not stated in this quote. The quote
      does not by itself separate neuroblastoma from nodular
      ganglioneuroblastoma, whose neuroblastoma component the review describes
      as often forming nodules in a background of intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma tissue. The passage restates an
      INPC criterion and reports no case data.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Ganglioneuroblastoma-Intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich): A transitional form between Neuroblastoma and Ganglioneuroma. Tumor is on its way toward full differentiation/maturation; however, the process is not complete, as evidenced by scattered \"residual\" microscopic neuroblastomatous foci presenting as pockets of neuropil containing varying numbers of neuroblastic cells with various stages of maturation including neuroblasts, differentiating neuroblasts, and/or ganglion cells. The proportion of the ganglioneuromatous component to \"residual\" neuroblastomatous foci should exceed 50% of the total volume in microscopic field(s) from representative section(s) of the tumor."
    explanation: >-
      Table I of this CCG/COG clinicopathological series restates the
      International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification definition of
      intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma: a transitional form between
      neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma whose maturation is incomplete, with
      scattered 'residual' microscopic neuroblastomatous foci presenting as
      pockets of neuropil, and a ganglioneuromatous component that should
      exceed 50% of the total volume in microscopic fields from representative
      sections. The quote states only the intermixed side of that threshold;
      read with the 2026 review's description of neuroblastoma as having
      limited or absent Schwannian stromal development, it is the quantitative
      criterion that sets the intermixed form apart from neuroblastoma. It is a
      diagnostic criterion restated from the INPC, not cohort data.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Ganglioneuroblastoma-Nodular (composite, Schwannian stroma-rich/stroma-dominant and stroma-poor): Composed of biologically different clones characterized by the presence of macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic neuroblastomatous nodule(s) of Schwannian stroma-poor component coexisting Ganglioneuroblastoma-intermixed of stroma-rich component or Ganglioneuroma of stroma-dominant component."
    explanation: >-
      The quoted sentence is the definition in the Ganglioneuroblastoma-Nodular
      row of Table I of this CCG/COG series, which restates the INPC
      definitions used to classify its centrally reviewed cases. A nodular
      tumor is composed of biologically different clones: one or more
      macroscopic, usually hemorrhagic, Schwannian stroma-poor
      neuroblastomatous nodules coexisting with stroma-rich intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma or stroma-dominant ganglioneuroma. The nodule is
      itself Schwannian stroma-poor, the stromal category of neuroblastoma, so
      what the definition adds is the coexisting stroma-rich or
      stroma-dominant component. It restates the INPC definition rather than
      reporting a finding of this cohort.
  - reference: PMID:37243318
    reference_title: "Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed: Clinicopathological implications of diagnosis at presentation and genomic correlations."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "In patients with stage M disease, GNBI noted at initial biopsy could not be corroborated in 67% samples examined at definitive resection. Thus, the definitive pathological biopsy diagnosis of GNBI in patients with concurrent metastatic disease requires high level of caution."
    explanation: >-
      In this retrospective Memorial Sloan Kettering series of 53 patients with
      intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) at diagnosis, 16 were first
      diagnosed at the center and the rest elsewhere before referral, and every
      included GNBI diagnosis was reviewed and confirmed at the center. All 12
      with INRG stage M disease had GNBI diagnosed on biopsy and received
      neoadjuvant chemotherapy before resection, and the resected tumor showed
      neuroblastoma in 8 and GNBI in 4. The authors therefore urge high caution
      with a biopsy diagnosis of GNBI when metastases are present, and recommend
      a note in the pathology report that the biopsy may not represent the whole
      tumor. The figure rests on 12 patients from one tertiary center, all given
      chemotherapy between biopsy and resection.
  - reference: PMID:37243318
    reference_title: "Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed: Clinicopathological implications of diagnosis at presentation and genomic correlations."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Of the 12 patients with metastatic disease, all of whom received neoadjuvant chemotherapy, surgery samples revealed GNBI in 4 (33%) and NB in 8 (67%)."
    explanation: >-
      In this retrospective Memorial Sloan Kettering series of patients with
      intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) at diagnosis, all 12 with
      metastatic disease received neoadjuvant chemotherapy, and the resected
      tumor showed neuroblastoma in 8 and GNBI in 4. The quoted sentence does
      not say how they were first diagnosed, but the paper's Table 2 shows that
      all 12 had GNBI diagnosed on biopsy. These are the same 12 patients, from
      one tertiary center, behind the authors' 67% figure and call for caution,
      so the sentence gives the underlying counts rather than independent
      support.
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "When tumor samples obtained by PNBC from one anatomic site shows either GNB-i or GN histology and the other anatomic site demonstrates NB histology in the same patient, we should consider (1) GNB-n where NB nodule present in the primary site is not sampled but found in the metastatic site (variant of GNB-n) ... or (2) an extremely rare example of multiple primaries presumably composed of different clones"
    explanation: >-
      This 2026 pathology review discusses percutaneous core needle biopsy and
      how the INPC is applied to it. It advises that when core needle biopsy
      samples from one site show intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or
      ganglioneuroma histology and another site in the same patient shows
      neuroblastoma, the pathologist should consider two readings. The first is
      a variant of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma: a neuroblastoma nodule in the
      primary tumor was not sampled, and the neuroblastoma is found at the
      metastatic site. The second, extremely rare, is multiple primaries
      presumably composed of different clones. For the first reading the review
      cites its reference for the 2003 modification of the INPC; for the second
      it cites a separate source. 'PNBC' is the review's misspelling of PCNB
      (percutaneous core needle biopsy), the abbreviation it uses elsewhere in
      the same paragraph. The sentence is an interpretive recommendation with
      citations, not case data.
treatments:
- name: Watchful Observation
  description: >-
    For non-metastatic intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma,
    studied together in retrospective series whose abstracts report no outcome
    for observed intermixed tumors on their own, a UK nationwide series
    concluded that watchful active observation can be a safe approach, with
    surgical resection reserved for symptomatic patients, and a single-center
    series that these tumors grow slowly and resection can carry significant
    morbidity, so watch-and-wait approaches should be considered for some
    patients. Observation rests on the biopsy diagnosis, which is not always
    upheld at surgery: in the UK series, 14 cases (12%) diagnosed on biopsy as
    ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma had neuroblastoma or
    nodular ganglioneuroblastoma on surgical pathology, and a small proportion
    of observed patients had their diagnosis revised at surgery.
  action_category: MONITORING
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: watchful active observation
    term:
      id: NCIT:C25598
      label: Observation Activity
  context: Non-metastatic intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (evidence pooled with ganglioneuroma)
  notes: >-
    NCIT:C15722 Patient Observation (synonyms include watchful waiting and
    active surveillance) fits the action more closely, but neither of its
    parents, Surveillance (NCIT:C15719) and Healthcare Activity (NCIT:C16205),
    is under NCIT:C25218, so the treatment-action enum rejects it. NCIT:C25598
    Observation Activity (watching something and taking note of what happens)
    reaches NCIT:C25218 only through Diagnostic Procedure (NCIT:C18020). It is
    bound here for the observation step of a monitoring strategy
    (action_category MONITORING), not for a diagnostic test; Acoustic_Neuroma
    and Infantile_Myofibromatosis bind it to their observation rows in the same
    way. NCIT:C61256 Monitoring and NCIT:C53607 Patient Monitoring are also
    outside NCIT:C25218.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:39582110
    reference_title: "Management of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma Intermixed: A United Kingdom Children's Cancer and Leukaemia Group (UK CCLG) Nationwide Study Report."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Watchful active observation can be a safe approach, with surgical resection reserved for symptomatic patients. However, a small proportion of patients in the observation group had their diagnosis revised to malignant at surgery."
    explanation: >-
      This UK CCLG nationwide retrospective review covers 259 patients with
      histologically confirmed non-metastatic ganglioneuroma or intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma. It concludes that observation can be safe, with
      resection reserved for symptomatic patients, though a small proportion of
      observed patients had their diagnosis revised at surgery. The abstract
      calls that a revision to malignant, and its background describes both
      tumors as benign variants, whereas ICD-O-3 codes ganglioneuroblastoma as
      malignant (9490/3). The two diagnoses are pooled and survival is not
      reported by management arm, so the safety statement is the authors'
      judgement. Twenty-one of the 58 observed patients (36%) later needed
      surgery, with progressive tumour growth cited as a reason; how many of
      them were reclassified is not stated.
  - reference: PMID:39582110
    reference_title: "Management of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma Intermixed: A United Kingdom Children's Cancer and Leukaemia Group (UK CCLG) Nationwide Study Report."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "In 45 cases (39%), surgical pathology was different from pathology at biopsy, and in 14 (12%) the pathology changed from GN/GNBi to neuroblastoma/GNB nodular."
    explanation: >-
      In this UK CCLG retrospective series of 259 patients with non-metastatic
      ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, surgical pathology
      differed from the biopsy diagnosis in 45 cases, and in 14 (12%) a biopsy
      diagnosis of ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma became
      neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma at surgery. The abstract
      gives no denominator for the percentages, does not split the changes by
      biopsy diagnosis or management arm, and does not say whether they reflect
      limited sampling, reinterpretation or change in the tumor before surgery.
  - reference: PMID:29369484
    reference_title: "Characteristics and management of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma-intermixed in children and adolescents."
    supports: SUPPORT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "GN and GNB-I have a slow growth rate and resection can be associated with significant morbidity. Watch and wait approaches should be considered for some GN and GNB-I."
    explanation: >-
      This single-centre retrospective cohort (Hospital for Sick Children,
      1990-2014) covers 67 patients with ganglioneuroma or intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma (17 intermixed), 21 of them observed. Its conclusion
      recommends considering watch-and-wait for some patients, citing slow
      tumour growth and the significant morbidity resection can carry. It
      supports this rationale and recommendation only; metastatic status is not
      stated, and 6 of the 21 observed patients had progressive disease.
      Pathology changed at resection in three cases, but the abstract says only
      that no ganglioneuroma became neuroblastoma, so it does not show the
      revisions to neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma reported in the
      UK series.
- name: Surgical Resection
  description: >-
    In localized intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, studied together with
    ganglioneuroma, chemotherapy seemed not to be effective and incomplete
    resection leaving only minor residuals (under 2 cm) appeared sufficient. In
    nodular ganglioneuroblastoma of the unfavorable subset, a CCG/COG series
    found no significant survival difference between complete primary tumor
    resection (at least 95% removed before chemotherapy or irradiation) and
    biopsy or partial resection, in patients with or without distant metastasis.
    The non-significant differences favored complete resection, the non-stage 4
    comparison included few incompletely resected patients, and prognosis
    depended significantly on stage.
  action_category: THERAPEUTIC
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: surgical resection
    term:
      id: NCIT:C164212
      label: Tumor Resection
  therapeutic_modality: SURGERY
  target_mechanisms:
  - target: Neoplastic Neuroblast Population
    treatment_effect: INHIBITS
    description: >-
      Resection removes all or most of the primary tumor together with its
      neoplastic neuroblasts. In a German series of localized intermixed tumors
      and ganglioneuroma, even incomplete resection leaving minor residuals
      (under 2 cm) appeared sufficient treatment. How resection affects outcome
      in nodular disease is described in this treatment's description.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:27465021
      reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "Outcome was excellent and chemotherapy seems not effective while incomplete resection with minor residuals (<2 cm) appears to be sufficient for treatment."
      explanation: >-
        In this retrospective study of localized ganglioneuroma and intermixed
        ganglioneuroblastoma registered in the German NB97 and NB2004 trials,
        the authors conclude that resection, even when it left residuals under 2
        cm, appears sufficient treatment, while chemotherapy seems not
        effective. This is a treatment outcome, so reading it as resection
        reducing the neoplastic neuroblast population is an inference. No tumor
        progressed after complete resection or with residuals under 2 cm, so
        control did not depend on removing the whole tumor. The sentence pools
        the two tumor types.
    - reference: PMID:27465021
      reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "However, patients with major residuals (>2 cm) had a worse EFS than patients with complete tumor resection or minor residuals as well for the whole group of mature NT (p < 0.001; Fig. 4a) as for the groups of GN (p = 0.001; Fig. 5a) and GNBI seen individually (p = 0.005; Fig. 6a)."
      explanation: >-
        In the same German series, patients left with residual tumor over 2 cm
        had worse event-free survival than those with complete resection or
        minor residuals, and this held for intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma on
        its own (p = 0.005). More tumor left behind went with more events: all
        five local progressions, two of them in intermixed tumors, arose in
        residuals over 2 cm. That this reflects resection reducing the tumor is
        an inference from outcome, and overall survival was not influenced by
        the extent of initial surgery.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Outcome was excellent and chemotherapy seems not effective while incomplete resection with minor residuals (<2 cm) appears to be sufficient for treatment."
    explanation: >-
      This summarises a retrospective study of localized tumours registered in
      the German neuroblastoma trials NB97 and NB2004, including 162
      ganglioneuroma and 55 intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) patients.
      None of the 11 patients given chemotherapy, 9 with GNBI, reached a
      complete or very good partial response. No tumour of either type
      progressed after complete resection or with residuals under 2 cm, whereas
      all five progressions had residuals over 2 cm. Few patients received
      chemotherapy, and the authors allow that single GNBI patients with
      immature regions in large residuals might benefit.
  - reference: PMID:41568977
    reference_title: "Is There a Role for Chemotherapy in Unresectable Ganglioneuroblastoma Intermixed?: A Case Series Analysis."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Four patients received chemotherapy but showed no significant tumor reduction postchemotherapy, and all required surgery afterward."
    explanation: >-
      This abstract reports a retrospective single-centre series of 12 patients
      with nonmetastatic intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma at Phoenix Children's
      Hospital. The four patients given chemotherapy showed no significant
      tumour reduction and all still needed surgery, and chemotherapy caused
      febrile neutropenia and an anaphylactic reaction. The title and conclusion
      frame these tumours as unresectable, but the abstract does not say why
      each patient received chemotherapy. With only four treated patients the
      evidence is thin, and it says nothing about how complete a resection must
      be.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Complete primary tumor resection was not beneficial to those GNB-N-US patients, regardless of whether metastasis was present or not."
    explanation: >-
      In a retrospective CCG/COG cohort, the authors report that complete
      primary resection was not beneficial in nodular unfavourable-subset
      ganglioneuroblastoma (GNB-N-US), with or without distant metastasis. The
      analysis compared complete resection (at least 95% removed before
      chemotherapy or irradiation) with biopsy or partial resection in 226
      patients otherwise treated by risk group. Differences were not significant
      but favoured complete resection (5-year EFS 83.4% vs 60.6%, p = 0.2227,
      non-stage 4, only 16 incompletely resected; 24.3% vs 5.4%, p = 0.1068,
      stage 4), so benefit is not excluded.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Complete resection: Complete or gross (≥95% of volume) tumor resection was achieved by single or multiple surgeries from the primary site before starting chemotherapy/irradiation therapy. ... the prognosis of patients with GNB-N-US was significantly dependent on clinical stage. ... For non-stage 4 patients, there was no significant difference in survival between patients with complete primary resection (85 cases) and with incomplete primary resection (16 cases), with 5-year EFS of 83.4% ± 9.1% vs. 60.6% ± 26.9% (p = 0.2227) and OS of 90.5% ± 7.2% vs. 80.0% ± 25.3% (p = 0.7176) ... The 5-year EFS for stage 4 patients with completely (74 cases) and incompletely resected (51 cases) primary tumors, respectively, was 24.3% ± 9.5% vs. 5.4% ± 5.2% (p = 0.1068) and OS was 38.5% ± 9.6% vs. 28.2% ± 11.9% (p = 0.5797)"
    explanation: >-
      Table II of this CCG/COG series defines complete resection as removal of
      at least 95% of the primary tumor before chemotherapy or irradiation; the
      comparison group had biopsy or partial resection. In nodular
      unfavorable-subset ganglioneuroblastoma, whose prognosis depended
      significantly on stage, neither event-free nor overall survival differed
      significantly by resection status, but every estimate favored complete
      resection: 5-year EFS 83.4% vs 60.6% in non-stage 4 patients, of whom only
      16 were incompletely resected, and 24.3% vs 5.4% in stage 4 patients (74
      vs 51). Resection after chemotherapy was not analysed, so the comparison
      does not address delayed surgery, and a benefit of complete resection is
      not excluded.
- name: Risk-Adapted Chemotherapy-Based Therapy
  description: >-
    Patients are treated by neuroblastoma risk group. In Children's Cancer Group
    and Children's Oncology Group studies that registered patients from 1991 to
    2006, low-risk patients had surgery alone, intermediate-risk patients
    moderate chemotherapy with surgery, and high-risk patients intensive
    chemotherapy, surgery, radiotherapy and often myeloablative therapy. In one
    referral series of intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma diagnosed at presentation
    (1995-2021), the 12 patients presenting with metastases, all diagnosed on
    biopsy, were treated as high-risk neuroblastoma with high-dose chemotherapy,
    radiotherapy and anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody immunotherapy. Of the 41 with
    locoregional disease, 11 received intermediate-risk neoadjuvant chemotherapy
    and 30 no cytotoxic therapy, and all 41 remained progression-free at a
    median follow-up of 4 years; in localized intermixed tumors chemotherapy has
    seemed ineffective (see Surgical Resection). Patients with nodular
    ganglioneuroblastoma older than 18 months with stage 4 disease form a
    high-risk subgroup that should be considered for aggressive upfront
    treatment.
  action_category: THERAPEUTIC
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: risk-adapted chemotherapy within neuroblastoma risk-group protocols
    term:
      id: NCIT:C15632
      label: Chemotherapy
  context: Ganglioneuroblastoma assigned to a neuroblastoma risk group, notably high-risk nodular disease
  notes: >-
    Bound to Chemotherapy, the component shared by the intermediate- and
    high-risk arms; the high-risk arm also includes surgery, radiotherapy,
    myeloablative therapy and, in the referral series, anti-GD2 immunotherapy,
    so the binding is narrower than the protocols this row describes. No
    therapeutic_agent is recorded, because no cited source names a regimen or
    antibody for ganglioneuroblastoma.

  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Among a total of 3,953 patients registered in CCG and COG neuroblastoma studies from August 1, 1991 to December 31, 2006, there were 552 cases whose diagnosis was either GN or GNB. ... Patients were treated by risk group: low-risk patients had surgery alone; intermediate-risk patients had a moderate chemotherapy treatment in addition to surgery; and high-risk patients had intensive chemotherapy, surgery, radiotherapy, and in many cases, myeloablative therapy."
    explanation: >-
      The Methods of this CCG/COG series of 552 ganglioneuroma and
      ganglioneuroblastoma cases, registered on neuroblastoma studies from
      August 1991 to December 2006, state how the patients were treated: by
      neuroblastoma risk group, with surgery alone for low risk, moderate
      chemotherapy with surgery for intermediate risk, and intensive
      chemotherapy, surgery, radiotherapy and often myeloablative therapy for
      high risk. These are the regimens of that period. It describes the
      protocols applied, not a comparison of treatments, and about 60% of the
      patients (329 of 552) were enrolled on therapeutic studies.
  - reference: PMID:22137163

      232 patients with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma among 4071 with a known
      INPC category. They conclude that, as in neuroblastoma overall, those
      older than 18 months with stage 4 disease are a high-risk subgroup who
      should be considered for aggressive upfront treatment. The recommendation
      follows from survival outcomes, not a comparison of treatments, and names
      no specific regimen. The abstract reports overall survival for all stage 4
      patients (35%, n=107), not separately for those over 18 months.
  - reference: PMID:37243318
    reference_title: "Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed: Clinicopathological implications of diagnosis at presentation and genomic correlations."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "All patients with metastatic disease were diagnosed at >18 months of age, were deemed to have high-risk NB, and were treated with multimodality therapy including high-dose chemotherapy, radiotherapy and anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody-mediated immunotherapy."
    explanation: >-
      In this retrospective Memorial Sloan Kettering series of intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma diagnosed at presentation (1995-2021), all 12
      patients with metastatic disease were older than 18 months, were treated
      as high-risk neuroblastoma, and received multimodality therapy including
      high-dose chemotherapy, radiotherapy and anti-GD2 monoclonal antibody
      immunotherapy. Table 2 shows that all 12 were diagnosed on biopsy, and 8
      of their 12 resections, after chemotherapy, showed neuroblastoma. The
      paper names no antibody and gives no treatment dates; it reports one
      center's practice, not a comparison of treatments.
  - reference: PMID:37243318
    reference_title: "Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed: Clinicopathological implications of diagnosis at presentation and genomic correlations."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Of the 41 patients with locoregional disease, 30 received no cytotoxic therapy and 11 received intermediate-risk neoadjuvant chemotherapy. All 41 remain alive progression-free at a median follow up of 4 years."
    explanation: >-
      In the same series, 11 of the 41 patients with locoregional intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma at diagnosis received intermediate-risk neoadjuvant
      chemotherapy and 30 received no cytotoxic therapy, and all 41 were alive
      and progression-free at a median follow-up of 4 years. This shows that
      some locoregional intermixed tumors are treated as intermediate risk. It
      is not a comparison of treatments, the paper reports no tumor response to
      chemotherapy, and the authors conclude that localized intermixed tumors do
      well even without cytotoxic therapy.
discussions:
- discussion_id: gnb_schwannian_stroma_origin
  prompt: >-
    Is the Schwannian stroma of ganglioneuroblastoma a non-neoplastic host
    population recruited by the neoplastic neuroblasts, or is it derived from
    the neoplastic clone?
  kind: CONTROVERSY
  status: OPEN
  attaches_to:
  - pathophysiology#Schwannian Stromal Development
  rationale: >-
    Evidence items use this convention: SUPPORT means the stroma is a
    non-neoplastic, recruited population; REFUTE means it derives from the
    neoplastic clone. For the non-neoplastic view, a 1996 study of maturing
    neuroblastomas found normal chromosome numbers in Schwann cells but abnormal
    numbers in neuroblastic and ganglionic cells, and an 8-case study of nodular
    ganglioneuroblastoma found the neuroblasts' MYCN gain in ganglion cells but
    not in Schwann cells. For the neoplastic view, a 2001 study of
    neuroblastomas found the same DNA content and GD2 staining in stromal and
    neuroblastic cells by laser-scanning cytometry in 20 tumors, and the same
    chromosomal makeup by fluorescence in situ hybridization in 7, and concluded
    that both derive from genetically identical neoplastic cells. A 2009
    cooperative-group paper describes the reports as controversial. The answer
    decides whether ploidy and copy-number results from stroma-rich tumors
    describe the tumor cells, and whether maturation is an interaction between
    tumor and host cells.
  posed_date: '2026-10-21T00:00:00Z'
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:8618605
    reference_title: "Role of ploidy, chromosome 1p, and Schwann cells in the maturation of neuroblastoma."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The normal number of chromosomes in Schwann cells and the abnormal number in neuroblastic ganglionic cells suggests that Schwann cells are a reactive population of normal cells that invade the neuroblastoma."
    explanation: >-
      In 20 maturing and mature neuroblastomas, chromosome counts by in situ
      hybridization found normal numbers in Schwann cells but abnormal numbers
      in neuroblastic and ganglionic cells. From this contrast the authors
      suggest that the Schwann cells are a reactive population of normal cells
      that invade the tumor, which supports the view that the stroma is a
      non-neoplastic host population. The evidence is chromosome number alone,
      which by itself cannot exclude a shared clonal origin, and the abstract
      does not say which tumors would now be classed as ganglioneuroblastoma.
  - reference: PMID:25081755
    reference_title: "The neuroblastoma and ganglion components of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma are genetically similar: evidence against separate clonal origins."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "In contrast, the Schwann cells have a different origin and may be derived from a non-neoplastic neural crest precursor."
    explanation: >-
      Supports the position that the Schwannian stroma is non-neoplastic rather
      than derived from the neoplastic clone. In eight nodular
      ganglioneuroblastomas studied by SNP array and in situ hybridization, the
      authors conclude that the Schwann cells, unlike the genetically related
      neuroblasts and ganglion cells, have a different origin, possibly a
      non-neoplastic neural crest precursor. Limitations: the Schwann cell
      result rests on in situ hybridization, because the arrays used DNA from
      whole neuroblastomatous and ganglioneuromatous areas, and the precursor
      origin is proposed, not traced.
  - reference: PMID:25081755
    reference_title: "The neuroblastoma and ganglion components of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma are genetically similar: evidence against separate clonal origins."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "By in situ hybridization, MYCN gain (4-10 gene copies/nucleus) was detected in 7/8 neuroblastoma samples. In ganglioneuromatous regions, gains were also detected in ganglion cells but not in Schwann cells."
    explanation: >-
      Supports the non-neoplastic position with the observation behind it. In
      this series of eight nodular ganglioneuroblastomas, in situ hybridization
      found MYCN gain in the neuroblastoma component of seven, and in
      ganglioneuromatous regions in ganglion cells but not in Schwann cells. The
      paper reports this as a gain of 4 to 10 copies per nucleus, not as
      amplification. Limitations: the series is small, and lacking the gain does
      not by itself show that Schwann cells arise outside the neoplastic clone,
      the point in dispute.
  - reference: PMID:11559566
    reference_title: "Neuroblastic and Schwannian stromal cells of neuroblastoma are derived from a tumoral progenitor cell."
    supports: REFUTE
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "In all 20 cases studied by Laser-Scanning Cytometry, SS cells identified by morphology and S-100 immunostaining had identical DNA content and GD2-staining pattern as their neuroblastic counterparts. In 7 cases, fluorescence in situ hybridization demonstrated the same chromosomal makeup for SS and neuroblastic cells. These results provide unequivocal evidence that neuroblastic and SS cells in NB are derived from genetically identical neoplastic cells and support the classical paradigm that NB arises from tumoral cells capable of development along multiple lineages."
    explanation: >-
      Laser-scanning cytometry of neuroblastoma touch imprints showed Schwannian
      stromal cells, identified by morphology and S-100 staining, with the same
      DNA content and GD2 staining as the neuroblastic cells in all 20 cases. In
      7 of the cases, bicolor fluorescence in situ hybridization on touch
      imprints from manually microdissected neuroblast and stroma-rich areas
      showed the same chromosomal makeup in the two populations. From their
      combined results, the authors concluded that both cell types derive from
      genetically identical neoplastic cells, supporting derivation of the
      stroma from the neoplastic clone rather than recruitment of non-neoplastic
      cells. The abstract gives no INPC category for the tumours, so applying
      this to ganglioneuroblastoma assumes the stroma has the same origin in
      both tumour types.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Though there have been controversial reports on cellular origin of Schwannian stromal cells (28) (29), Ambros et al. report that Schwannian cells in GN-M and GNB-I are not neoplastic and do not share the same cellular origin of neural crest with neuroblastic cells, but they are normal diploid cells recruited by biologically favorable hyperdiploid neuroblastic cells."
    explanation: >-
      In a Children's Cancer Group/Children's Oncology Group clinicopathological
      study of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma, the authors note
      controversial reports on the cellular origin of Schwannian stromal cells,
      then restate Ambros et al.'s report that, in ganglioneuroma-maturing and
      intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, these cells are not neoplastic but normal
      diploid cells recruited by biologically favourable hyperdiploid
      neuroblastic cells. The sentence records the disagreement and supports the
      non-neoplastic, recruited-stroma position. It restates another group's
      work without new data, and states this only for those two categories, not
      for nodular ganglioneuroblastoma.
- discussion_id: gnb_nodule_clonality
  prompt: >-
    Is the neuroblastomatous nodule of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma a clone
    genetically distinct from the rest of the tumor, or a part of a single
    neoplastic clone that differs from the rest only in being less
    differentiated?
  kind: CONTROVERSY
  status: OPEN
  attaches_to:
  - pathophysiology#Schwannian Stroma-Poor Neuroblastomatous Nodule
  - has_subtypes#Nodular
  rationale: >-
    Evidence items use this convention: SUPPORT means the nodule is clonally
    distinct (a multiclonal tumor), that is, a genetically distinct clone,
    whether it arose independently or as a subclone of a common ancestor; REFUTE
    means the nodule's neuroblasts and the ganglion cells of the
    ganglioneuromatous component belong to one clone and differ only in
    differentiation. A 2026 pathology review of the INPC describes nodular
    tumors as multi-clonal, and a 1993 single-case study found that the more
    differentiated portion and the neuroblastic foci of one composite
    ganglioneuroblastoma differed in DNA content and MYCN copy number. An 8-case
    genetic study found the nodule neuroblasts and the ganglion cells
    genetically related and concluded that the behavior of nodular tumors cannot
    be explained by separate clones with distinct genetic signatures; that
    argues against independent clonal origins but does not exclude a subclone
    that evolved from a common ancestor.
  posed_date: '2026-10-21T00:00:00Z'
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Tumors in this category are considered as multi-clonal tumors. In gross appearance, the NB component often forms hemorrhagic and necrotic nodule(s) in a background of elastic and yellowish white component of GNB-i or GN"
    explanation: >-
      In its account of the INPC categories, this 2026 pathology review states
      that tumours in 'this category' are considered multi-clonal, and that the
      neuroblastoma component often forms haemorrhagic, necrotic nodules within
      a ganglioneuroblastoma-intermixed or ganglioneuroma background, which
      identifies the category as nodular ganglioneuroblastoma. It therefore
      supports the multiclonal (clonally distinct nodule) position. It is an
      expert classification statement with no clonality data, so it records how
      these tumours are classified rather than showing that the nodule is a
      separate clone.
  - reference: PMID:8108298
    reference_title: "Evidence for molecular heterogeneity in human ganglioneuroblastoma."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "We analyzed a composite ganglioneuroblastoma for N-myc copy number at initial resection and 2 years later after progressive disease. In the second sample the more differentiated portion of the tumor was analyzed separately from the neuroblastic foci for N-myc copy number and DNA ploidy. The DNA content and N-myc copy number differed in the two morphologically discrete areas of the tumor, suggesting that at least two clones were present."
    explanation: >-
      This case report analysed one composite ganglioneuroblastoma (the older
      name for the nodular subtype) at initial resection and again 2 years
      later, after progressive disease. In the second sample, the more
      differentiated portion and the neuroblastic foci differed in DNA content
      and N-myc (MYCN) copy number, which the authors read as at least two
      clones, supporting the clonally distinct position. It is one case, and the
      abstract does not say whether stromal cells were separated from tumour
      cells, so the two clones are an inference from two tissue portions. The
      abstract reports the separate analysis of the two areas only for the
      sample taken after progression, so it does not show whether they already
      differed at initial resection.
  - reference: PMID:25081755
    reference_title: "The neuroblastoma and ganglion components of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma are genetically similar: evidence against separate clonal origins."
    supports: REFUTE
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Our results suggest that the clinical behavior of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma cannot be explained by the presence of separate clones with distinct genetic signatures."
    explanation: >-
      Analysing eight nodular ganglioneuroblastomas by SNP array and in situ
      hybridization, with DNA extracted separately from neuroblastomatous and
      ganglioneuromatous areas, the authors suggest that the clinical behaviour
      of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma cannot be explained by separate clones
      with distinct genetic signatures. This favours the single-clone position,
      arguing against independent clonal origins for the neuroblastoma nodule
      and the ganglion cells of the ganglioneuromatous component. It neither
      covers the Schwann cells, which the same abstract assigns a different
      origin, nor excludes a nodule subclone diverging from a common ancestor;
      the series is small.
- discussion_id: gnb_tmm_prognostic_significance
  prompt: >-
    Does telomere maintenance mechanism status predict outcome in
    ganglioneuroblastoma, as it does in high-risk neuroblastoma?
  kind: KNOWLEDGE_GAP
  status: OPEN
  attaches_to:
  - pathophysiology#Telomere Maintenance Mechanism Activation
  rationale: >-
    The series that established telomere maintenance in ganglioneuroblastoma
    says its clinical significance there is not yet assessable, because the
    cohort needs several more years of follow-up. This matters for how the
    pathophysiology node is read. In high-risk neuroblastoma, telomere
    maintenance is a poor-prognostic marker and is proposed for the next
    International Neuroblastoma Risk Group classification, so the temptation is
    to carry that prognostic meaning across to ganglioneuroblastoma on the
    strength of the shared mechanism and the nodular subtype's comparable
    positive rate. Nothing in the ganglioneuroblastoma data supports that step
    yet. This gap is why the node has no edge to an outcome or risk node, and
    why one should not be added from neuroblastoma evidence.
  posed_date: '2026-10-28T00:00:00Z'
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41858373
    reference_title: "Telomere maintenance mechanisms are activated in ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "a solid evaluation of the clinical significance of TMM status in GN and GNB will require several additional years of long-term follow-up."
    explanation: >-
      The authors state the gap themselves, in their discussion, about their own
      cohort. It is what makes this an absence of evidence rather than a
      negative result: they do not report that telomere maintenance fails to
      predict outcome in ganglioneuroblastoma, only that their follow-up is too
      short to say.

Evaluations and curation notes (1)

Create: Ganglioneuroblastoma (intermixed and nodular subtypes) · 2026-10-21T19:15:16Z · View source

New entry for ganglioneuroblastoma (MONDO:0005035; NCIT:C3790 exact match), the peripheral neuroblastic tumour between neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma, covering both INPC categories as has_subtypes: Intermixed (MONDO:0003326; NCIT:C42057) and Nodular (MONDO:0003325; NCIT:C42058). Scope: MONDO and NCIT define the class by a Schwannian stroma making up more than half of the tumour volume, which the INPC applies only to the intermixed category; the description and the MONDO mapping justification say so. Central nervous system ganglioneuroblastoma (NCIT:C92629) and Hirschsprung disease-ganglioneuroblastoma syndrome (MONDO:0013082) are out of scope. Content: INPC definitions of both categories, with the rule that the favourable/unfavourable grouping is made on tissue taken before chemotherapy or irradiation (both histopathology findings, NCIT:C42062 and NCIT:C42066); a diagnosis row (NCIT:C18190 Histopathologic Examination) for the whole-tumour ideal and the limited-material reporting practice; biopsy-resection concordance in localized intermixed tumours; the nodular favourable/unfavourable criteria, their origin (Umehara 2000, confirmed by the INPC committee in 2003), the metastatic-site variant, and INRG and CCG/COG outcomes; subtype outcomes. Four pathophysiology nodes (neoplastic neuroblasts; maturation into ganglion cells; Schwannian stromal development; the stroma-poor nodule) with evidenced edges, including in vitro Schwann cell and neuroblast crosstalk (conditioned medium, EGFL8, PEDF). Phenotypes: abdominal and posterior mediastinal mass, distant metastasis at diagnosis (nodular; HP:0002664 with NO_HPO_TERM and a term gap), congenital Horner syndrome (Orphanet), elevated urinary HVA and VMA in intermixed tumours (German NB97/NB2004, Table 1). Biochemical: NSE and serum ferritin (He 2017). Genetic: MYCN amplification as a rare somatic biomarker, with the single-centre 14% nodular figure explained as one amplified tumour. Treatments: watchful observation, tumour resection, risk-adapted chemotherapy within neuroblastoma risk-group protocols. Discussions: two CONTROVERSY items, the origin of the Schwannian stroma and the clonality of the nodule, each with a stated SUPPORT/REFUTE convention. Not included, on purpose: the ALK germline row Orphanet lists, because how a tumour entry links to a separate predisposition disease (MONDO:0013083) is the open question in #14976; the entry notes say so. No stages: block and no invasion_and_metastasis conformance: ganglioneuroblastoma is staged with the neuroblastoma INSS/INRGSS systems, whose stage definitions are in Neuroblastoma.yaml, and no node here describes dissemination. MIBG uptake, intraspinal extension, stridor, superior vena cava syndrome, VIP-related diarrhoea, hypertension and opsoclonus-myoclonus are left for a later clinical-features pass. Sources: a claude_code deep-research report (research/Ganglioneuroblastoma-deep-research-claude_code.md) used as leads; every evidence item quotes the cached abstract or full text. Cooperative-group overlaps (Umehara, Peuchmaur and Okamatsu; INRG with COG) are stated in the explanations. Before the PR, a four-lens pre-review with two adversarial verifiers per finding upheld 28 of 36 findings (23 by both verifiers); their fixes were applied, except the ALK row described above. Validation: just validate, validate-terms, count-verified-snippets (83/83), validate-disorders (83 snippets, 83 titles, 0 issues), prose-figure audit with no unsupported figure, and the whole-KB offline gates. 83 evidence items from 23 references.

Ganglioneuroblastoma (MONDO:0005035) — Comprehensive Disease Characteristics Research Report
claude-haiku-4-5-20251001, claude-opus-5[1m] 79 citations 2026-10-21T15:08:14.581940

Ganglioneuroblastoma (MONDO:0005035) — Comprehensive Disease Characteristics Research Report

Prepared: 21 October 2026 Scope: Literature and ontology survey for knowledge-base population Evidence-scope caveat (read first): Ganglioneuroblastoma (GNB) is not an independently studied disease in most of the primary literature. It is one of four categories in a single nosological family — the peripheral neuroblastic tumours (pNTs) — and the overwhelming majority of mechanistic, genomic and therapeutic evidence is generated on neuroblastoma cohorts in which GNB cases are either pooled or excluded. Throughout this report, claims are labelled [GNB-specific] where the cited study separately analysed GNB, and [pNT-wide / extrapolated] where the evidence is from neuroblastoma and is applied to GNB by family membership. This distinction matters a great deal for a mechanism knowledge base: the two GNB subtypes sit at opposite ends of the clinical spectrum (one is a benign-behaving tumour managed by observation, the other is risk-stratified and treated exactly like neuroblastoma), and lumping them produces statements that are false of both.

1. Disease Information

1.1 Overview

Ganglioneuroblastoma is a neuroblastic tumour of the developing sympathetic nervous system occupying the middle of a maturational spectrum that runs from undifferentiated neuroblastoma (malignant, Schwannian stroma-poor) through to ganglioneuroma (benign, Schwannian stroma-dominant). It is defined histologically, not clinically or genetically: the diagnosis requires neuroblastic cells, ganglion cells, and Schwannian stroma, with the stromal component exceeding 50% of tumour volume.

MONDO's definition (verified via EBI OLS4, MONDO:0005035):

"A neuroblastic tumor characterized by the presence of neuroblastic cells, ganglion cells, and a stroma with Schwannian differentiation constituting more than fifty-percent of the tumor volume."

The single most important fact about this entry is that "ganglioneuroblastoma" names two biologically and clinically distinct entities, and the WHO 5th-edition classification keeps them separate rather than as subtypes of one thing:

Entity ICD-O-3.2 Schwannian stroma Behaviour
Ganglioneuroma 9490/0 Stroma-dominant Benign
Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (GNBi) 9490/3 Stroma-rich Favourable histology at any age
Neuroblastoma 9500/3 Stroma-poor Age/MKI/grade-dependent
Ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (GNBn) (and other composite neuroblastic tumours) 9490/3 Composite: stroma-rich/dominant + stroma-poor Graded by the highest-grade nodule; can be fully malignant

Source: WHO Classification of Tumours Editorial Board (2023), Paediatric Tumours, WHO Classification of Tumours 5th edition, Volume 7, IARC, Lyon — reproduced in Table 1 of the ICCR Neuroblastoma Histopathology Reporting Guide, 1st ed. v1.0, November 2023 (ISBN 978-1-922324-39-9), https://www.iccr-cancer.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/11/ICCR-Neuroblastoma-1st-ed-v1.0-bookmark.pdf

Verbatim definitions from the ICCR dataset (Note 7, Histological tumour type):

Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich) category — "Tumours in this category are characterised by the presence of a ganglioneuromatous component with Schwannian stromal development (see below) exceeding 50% of the tissue, where microscopic nests of neuroblastoma are present in an intermixed or randomly distributed pattern. In those microscopic nests, the tumour cells are in various stages of differentiation (often predominantly differentiating neuroblasts) with clearly identifiable naked neuritic processes (neuropile, not covered by Schwann cells) around their cytoplasm. Grossly visible haemorrhagic/necrotic nodules suggestive of aggressive neuroblastomatous growth are absent. Tumours in this category are histologically one step behind the final stage of complete maturation towards ganglioneuroma."

Ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite, Schwannian stroma-rich/stroma-dominant and stroma-poor) category — "Tumours in this category are composed of multiple clones demonstrating distinct histologies: one or more Neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor) nodules set within a background of either Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich) or Ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant). The neuroblastoma nodules are usually grossly identifiable and often haemorrhagic/necrotic and fragile. In contrast, the Ganglioneuroblastoma, Intermixed/Ganglioneuroma component is tan-yellow and solid/elastic."

Not verified in this session (flagged for lookup before binding): the ICD-11 morphology code reported by aggregator sources as XH77W7 (ICD-11 Foundation 1916269207) was obtained from MalaCards/Wikidata-derived search summaries rather than from the WHO ICD-11 browser directly. Do not bind it without checking the ICD-11 MMS browser.

A curation note: NCIT:C92629 (Central Nervous System Ganglioneuroblastoma) and NCIT:C124272 name a different tumour — a CNS neuroepithelial neoplasm — that shares the word "ganglioneuroblastoma" but is not a peripheral neuroblastic tumour. Binding it into a pNT entry would be a Named Entity Confusion of exactly the kind this knowledge base's reference SOP warns about. Olfactory/esthesioneuroblastoma-associated "olfactory ganglioneuroblastoma" is likewise a separate entity.

1.3 Synonyms

From MONDO: ganglioneuroblastoma (disease), ganglioneuroblastoma, malignant, ganglioneuroblastoma (morphologic abnormality). In the literature: GNB, GNBi / GNB-I (intermixed), GNBn / GNB-N (nodular), composite ganglioneuroblastoma, stroma-rich neuroblastoma (historical, pre-INPC). The umbrella family term is peripheral neuroblastic tumour (pNT); "neuroblastoma" is widely used generically for the whole family, which is a recurring source of data-extraction error.

1.4 Source of information

Predominantly aggregated disease-level resources: histopathology reference series (INPC committee reviews), international cooperative-group registries (INRG Data Commons, COG ANBL00B1 biology study), and population cancer registries (SEER). Individual-patient EHR-derived data are essentially absent from the GNB literature; the SEER-derived analyses are registry abstractions, not EHR phenotyping. A large fraction of the GNB-specific literature consists of single case reports and small institutional case series, which should be weighted accordingly.

2. Etiology

2.1 Disease causal factors

GNB is a sporadic somatic neoplasm of embryonic origin in the overwhelming majority of cases. There is no established environmental or infectious cause, and no single necessary-and-sufficient genetic lesion. The accepted model is that pNTs arise from failure of developmentally programmed differentiation and/or death of sympathoadrenal progenitors derived from the neural crest, with the degree of residual differentiation capacity determining where on the neuroblastoma → GNB → ganglioneuroma spectrum a tumour lands.

"Neuroblastoma (NB), a heterogenous pediatric tumor of the sympathetic nervous system, is the most common and deadly extracranial solid malignancy diagnosed in infants." — Sainero-Alcolado L, Sjöberg Bexelius T, Santopolo G, et al. Defining neuroblastoma: From origin to precision medicine. Neuro Oncol. 2024. PMID: 39101440

2.2 Genetic risk factors

Germline predisposition is rare and is shared with neuroblastoma rather than specific to GNB. The authoritative recent statement is the 2023 AACR Childhood Cancer Predisposition Workshop update:

"Genetic predisposition to neuroblastoma (NB) is relatively rare. Only 1% to 2% of patients have a family history of NB, 3% to 4% of cases present with bilateral or multifocal primary tumors, and occasional patients have syndromes that are associated with increased NB risk. Previously, a germline pathogenic variant (GPV) in PHOX2B was associated with Hirschsprung disease and congenital central hypoventilation syndrome. Recently, certain GPVs were shown to be responsible for congenital central hypoventilation syndrome and NB predisposition. Also, several groups determined that activating GPVs in ALK accounted for a substantial number of familial NB." — Kamihara J, Diller LR, Foulkes WD, et al. Neuroblastoma Predisposition and Surveillance—An Update from the 2023 AACR Childhood Cancer Predisposition Workshop. Clin Cancer Res. 2024;30(15):3137. PMID: 38860978; DOI: 10.1158/1078-0432.CCR-24-0237

The ICCR dataset states the same figure and names the broader syndrome set:

"An inherited predisposition to neuroblastoma has been estimated in 1-2% of patients with newly diagnosed neuroblastic tumours... The genes most commonly altered in inherited neuroblastoma are ALK and PHOX2B. Other cancer predisposition syndromes (CPS) that may include neuroblastic tumours are RASopathy syndromes, Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome, Li-Fraumeni syndrome and DNA repair disorders." (ICCR Note 1)

Gene HGNC Role Key evidence
ALK hgnc:427 Major familial NB gene; activating germline missense in tyrosine kinase domain (R1275Q, F1174L, R1192P, G1128A); autosomal dominant, incomplete penetrance Mossé YP, et al. Identification of ALK as a major familial neuroblastoma predisposition gene. Nature. 2008;455(7215):930-935. PMID: 18724359; Janoueix-Lerosey I, et al. Nature. 2008;455(7215):967-970
PHOX2B hgnc:9143 First gene shown to predispose to NB; homeodomain variants; co-segregates with Hirschsprung disease and CCHS Trochet D, et al. Am J Hum Genet. 2004. PMID: 15024693 — "PHOX2B, therefore, stands as the first gene for which germline mutations predispose to NB"; Mossé YP, et al. Am J Hum Genet. 2004. PMID: 15338462; Bourdeaut F, et al. Cancer Lett. 2005 (R100L). PMID: 15949893
NF1 hgnc:7765 RASopathy; a GNB case with a heterozygous NF1 exon 1–58 deletion is reported Zhang Q, et al. Ganglioneuroblastoma associated with neurofibromatosis type 1: a case report with a systematic review. Front Oncol. 2026. PMID: 42311262
LMO1 hgnc:6641 Common-variant susceptibility via super-enhancer polymorphism rs2168101 G>T altering GATA binding Oldridge DA, et al. Genetic predisposition to neuroblastoma mediated by a LMO1 super-enhancer polymorphism. Nature. 2015. PMID: 26560027

ALKAL1 (hgnc:33775) / ALKAL2 (hgnc:27683) encode the ALK ligands (ALKAL/FAM150/AUGβ) and are mechanistically relevant to ALK activation, though not established predisposition genes.

[GNB-specific gap] No study has tested whether germline predisposition is enriched, depleted, or differently distributed in GNBi versus GNBn versus neuroblastoma. The 1–2% figure is a pNT-wide estimate.

2.3 Environmental risk factors

No environmental exposure has been established as causal. Age (infancy/early childhood), and to a lesser degree ancestry, are the only consistently reported non-genetic risk associations for pNTs.

From NCI PDQ (Neuroblastoma Treatment, Health Professional Version): relative risks of neuroblastoma compared with White patients were 0.54 (Hispanic), 0.64 (Asian/Pacific Islander), 0.69 (American Indian/Alaska Native), and 0.73 (Black).

In the SEER GNB cohort (n = 1,194), racial distribution was White 78.8%, Black 12.6%, other 8.5% — a distribution that reflects both incidence and the underlying US population, and should not be read as a risk estimate (Li W, et al. Heliyon. 2024;10(9):e30891. PMID: 38774105).

Not applicable / no evidence found: occupational exposure, radiation, pollution, tobacco, diet, and alcohol have no established role in GNB specifically. Parental-exposure epidemiology for neuroblastoma has produced no replicated finding of the strength needed for a mechanism claim.

2.4 Protective factors

Genetic: No validated protective variant is reported for GNB. The protective end of the ALK allelic spectrum is simply absence of an activating variant, which is not a protective allele.

Environmental/behavioural: None established. Population newborn screening is explicitly not protective and the evidence is strong and negative — see §13.

2.5 Gene–environment interactions

No established gene–environment interaction for GNB. There is a conceptually important gene–developmental-timing interaction rather than a gene–environment one: the prognostic effect of histology is age-dependent (see §3 and §8), and the prognostic effect of MKI has distinct age cut-offs per MKI class (Teshiba R, et al. Pediatr Dev Pathol. 2014;17(6):441-449. PMID: 25207821). This is interaction between a tumour-intrinsic variable and host developmental stage, and should be modelled as such rather than as an environmental modifier.

3. Phenotypes

3.1 How GNB presents

Most phenotypes are mass-effect phenotypes determined by anatomical site, not by tumour biology. A second, smaller group are secretory/paraneoplastic phenotypes. This split is the right organising principle for a mechanism entry: the first group's causal parent is tumour growth at a site, the second's is a secreted product or an anti-neural immune response.

The ICCR dataset's statement of the paraneoplastic set (Note 1, verbatim):

"Multiple paraneoplastic syndromes can be associated with neuroblastic tumours, including opsoclonus myoclonus ataxia, cerebellar ataxia, Horner syndrome, Hirschsprung disease/CCHS, rapid-onset obesity with hypoventilation, hypothalamic dysfunction, autonomic dysregulation (ROHHAD), and vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP)-associated secretory diarrhoea or VIPoma syndrome."

3.2 Phenotype table with suggested HPO terms

3.3 Phenotype characteristics

  • Age of onset. Early childhood. GNB subtypes are consistently older at diagnosis than neuroblastoma: median 28.9 months (NB) vs 38.4 months (GNBn) vs 47.5 months (GNBi) in a 279-patient single-centre series (He WG, et al. Oncotarget. 2017;8(23):37730-37739. PMID: 28465480). In the INRG GNBn cohort, 83% were older than 18 months (Angelini P, et al. Eur J Cancer. 2012. PMID: 22137163).
  • Severity. Bimodal by subtype. GNBi is almost uniformly indolent; GNBn spans the full range, with stage 4 GNBn carrying 35% ± 7% OS (PMID: 22137163).
  • Progression. GNBi/GN grow slowly and may be static for years: "GN and GNB-I have a slow growth rate and resection can be associated with significant morbidity" (Alexander N, et al. Pediatr Blood Cancer. 2018. PMID: 29369484). GNBn behaves as the biology of its nodule dictates.
  • Frequency among affected individuals. Reliable per-phenotype frequencies for GNB specifically are not published. The serum/urine marker frequencies in §3.2 (from PMID: 28465480) are the best available and are from a single centre with n = 20 GNBn and n = 31 GNBi; treat them as indicative, not as population frequencies.

3.4 Quality-of-life impact

No GNB-specific QoL instrument data exist. The relevant data are from high-risk neuroblastoma survivors, which applies to the GNBn stage-4 subgroup and not to GNBi:

  • The COG LEAHRN cross-sectional cohort found moderate-to-severe hearing loss in 72% of high-risk neuroblastoma survivors, with more than half underweight, and most participants carrying at least two clinically important late effects (Lancet Child Adolesc Health, 2025; https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S235246422500241X).
  • Flaadt T, et al. Long-Term Outcomes and Quality of Life of High-Risk Neuroblastoma Patients Treated with a Multimodal Treatment Including Anti-GD2 Immunotherapy. Cancers. 2025;17(1):149. PMID: 39796776 — among 25 survivors at 25 years: ototoxicity 64%, cardiac toxicity 24%, endocrine effects 76%; "Quality-of-life scores exceeded matched general population comparators."
  • Self-reported survivor data show 50% meeting anxiety/depression criteria versus a 25% population norm (PubMed 37311662).

Instruments in use: EQ-5D, SF-36, PROMIS, PedsQL. No disease-specific GNB instrument exists.

4. Genetic / Molecular Information

4.1 There is no causal gene for ganglioneuroblastoma

This is a structural point that should be recorded explicitly rather than left as an absence. GNB is a somatic neoplasm defined by morphology; it has driver lesions, not a causal gene, and a given GNB may carry none of the recurrent drivers at all. The diagnosis is made on a histology slide, and the molecular work-up is prognostic rather than diagnostic.

4.2 Recurrent somatic alterations

Alteration Gene / locus Frequency Origin Functional consequence Evidence
MYCN amplification (double minutes → HSRs) MYCN, hgnc:7559, 2p24 GNBi 0%; GNBn 14%; NB 26% in one series (PMID: 28465480); 2% of GNBn in the INRG cohort (PMID: 22137163) Somatic Amplification ⇒ N-Myc overexpression; "a proto-oncogene which inhibits cell differentiation and apoptosis and promotes division" (ICCR Note 13). FISH: ratio ≥4 = amplified; >1 but <3 = "gain", clinical significance unclear ICCR Note 13; PMID: 28465480; PMID: 22137163
ALK point mutation ALK, hgnc:427, 2p23 "mutated in 8-10% of sporadic neuroblastic tumours, amplified in about 2%" (ICCR Note 13). In 943 patients: 10.5% at diagnosis, "highest frequencies in stage 4 patients <18 months"; at relapse the frequency rises ~70%, mostly de novo R1275Q Somatic (also germline — §2.2) Gain of function: constitutive receptor tyrosine kinase activity.
